Dr. Hornbeck has asked me to send to you
the attached single-page memorandum of Octo-
ber 31 entitled "Exports of Scrap Iron to
Japan", in response to your request under
reference. The only references in the cor-
respondence between Mrs. Roosevelt and Mrs.
Steiger to official figures are to our exports
of scrap iron to Japan. Consequently, it is
believed that the attached memorandum meets
Mrs. Roosevelt's request for the "facts".

DRAFT OF A REPLY
October 31, 1940
From:
Mr. Welles.
To:
The President.
In response to your memorandum of October 22
requesting the preparation of a memorandum of reply
to Mrs. Roosevelt's request for facts on the subject
of exports of scrap iron to Japan, I am enclosing a
brief memorandum which I believe contains the essential
relevant facts.
Under date of September 30, I sent to Mrs. Roosevelt
a more elaborate memorandum on the subject of United
States-Japanese trade relations since the outbreak of the
Sino-Japanese hostilities. I believe that some of the
material in that memorandum may also be of interest to
her in connection with her correspondence with Mrs.
Steiger.

=== Page 9 === (confidence 0.97)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
August 25, 1939
Batavia's air mail despatch no. 312,
August 8, 1939, summarizes the opinions of
highly-placed Notherlands Indian Government
officials in regard to the question of what
action the Netherlands Indies would take if the
United States placed an embargo on shipments,
inter alia, of petroleum, tin and nickel to
Japan.
The consensus of Netherlands Indies of-
ficial opinion is that the Netherlands Indies
would continue to sell these products to Japan
1n order to protect itself from attack by
Japan sinoe Japan would no doubt consider a
refusal to sell as a casus belli and would move
to occupy the oil fields in Borneo and Sumatra.
Should the United States impose an embargo
on oil shipments to Japan, we will find our-
selves in the anomalous position of compelling
an American-owned corporation in the Netherlands
Indies (a subsidiary af Socony yacuum) to supply
Japan with some of the oll which we would deny
to that country.
Nevertheless Mr. Dickover favors an embargo.
894.24/708
MR. HUENBEL

AMERICAN CONSULATE GENERAL
Airmail
Batavia, Java, August 8, 1939.
STRICTLY CONFIDENTIAL
SUBJECT:
OPINION IN THE NETHERLANDS INDIES IN REGARD
1-1403
TO THE PROPOSAL IN THE UNITED STATES
TO ENBARGO SHIPLENTS OF WAR
σ
MATERIALS TO JAPAN.
ue
2
THE HONORABLE
S.
THE SECRETARY OF STATE,
WASHINGTON.
SIR:
v9
I have the honor to submit the folloving report
on opinion in the Netherlands Indies in regard to the
894.24/708
growing movement in the United States (as indicated
by the resolution introluced into the Senate by Senat-
or Pittman and the recent notice of abrogation of the
191l treaty with Japan) to place an embargo on the
sale of materials of war, especially iron and steel,
2M
scrap metals and petroleum, to nations violating
treaties to which the United States is a party. It
is believed in the Netherlands Indies -- obviously
with reason -- that the movement is directed primari-
ly against Japan, and as the Netherlands Indies have
certain close trade relationships with Japan, the
possible repercussions of such an embargo on the Ne-
therlands Indies have been arousing considerable in-
terest among the Netherlands officials here.
The Netherlands Indies is not an iron and steel
ILED
producing country and consequently that phase of the
question is not of interest to the local officials.
The country does, however, produce large quantities
of

=== Page 11 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
of petroleum and consi lerable juantities of tin and
nickel ore, all of which would be desired by Japan
should an embargo be placed on shipments of oil end
base and serap metals fron the United States. I
learned recently that the Netherlands Indian Govern-
ment had been giving some thought to the policies which
the country should adopt in the event of the American
embargo becoming effective and consequently I took oc-
casion, as opoortunity oftered, to liscuss the matter
informally with highly-placed Netherlands Indian Gov-
ernment officials. Their comarente are sumarized be-
low.
General Boerstra, Commandant of the Netherlands
Indian Army. stated that an embargo on the sale of oil
fron the United States to Japan contained an element
of danger to the Netherlands Indies, as Japan might,
on the ground of "military necessity", try to seize
and occupy the oil fields of the Netherlands Indies.
But, stated the General, this danger would be avoided
if the Netherlands Indies should sell to Japan all the
oil needed. In his opinion, the British Government
would not object to such sale, even should Great Brit-
ain be engaged in war with Japan. It is greatly to
the advantage of Great Britain to maintain the neutral-
ity of the Netherlands Indies, because, should the
Netherlands Indies engage in unneutral acts, the Jap-
anese could fairly easily occupy parts of the archi-
pelago and use them as bases from which to attack
Singapore and Australia. Therefore Great Britain
w111

=== Page 12 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
will make every efrort to keep the Netherlands Indies
neutral in any conflict, even to the extent of agree-
ing to the sale of war supplies to the enemy.
Mr. Lovink, the Advisor on East Asiatic Affairs
to the Netherlands Indian Government, holds the same
opinion. He saii that the Netherlands Indies, in self-
protection, would be compelled to sell oil to Japan
should the United States place an embargo on shipments
of oil to that country. A refusal to sell oil might
well be considered a "cesus belli", resulting in Jap-
anese occupation of the oil fiolds of Borneo and Suma-
tra. Japan might even go so far, said Mr. Lovink, as
to demand that the en ire output of certain fields, such
as those at Tarakan and Balikpapan in eastern Borneo.
be delivered to Japanese tankers, and the Netherlands
Indies could not refuse, although the oil is needed for
their own refineries and their export trade in refined
petroleum products. The Government, said Mr. Lovink, is
giving thought to this question. Mr. Lovink also said
that a retired Japanese Admiral travels around the world
looking into the oil situation in each country. He was
in the Netherlands Indies not long ago and told Japanese
friends (the information came to Mr. Lovink indirectly)
that the oil fields at Tarakan and Balikpapan were
"most important" to the Japanese.
Captain van Staveren, the Chief-of-Staff of the
Netherlands Navy in Indian waters, said that the Nether-
lands Indian Government is giving serious thought to the
possible consequences of an embargo on shipments of oil
from

=== Page 13 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
from the United States to Japan.
The Government real-
izes that the danger exists that Japan may become des-
perate and do anything to obtain necessary war supplies.
Therefore the Netherlands Indies will be compelled to
supply Japan with oil, in order to protect itself from
attack. Janan is now buying only about 40,000 tons of
oil per month, as compared with 130,000 tons per month
formerly purchased (Captain van Staveren must be in er-
ror in this statement; it cannot be possible that Japan,
by rationing petroleuu products, has decreased its con-
sumption of oil by 75 per cent) and the Netherlands In-
dies can easily supply this amount of crude petroleum.
Moreover, it would not pay Janan to take the Borneo oil
fields. Tarakan and Balikpapan are fairly well protected
with army posts and air-fields, end the Japanese would
have a long line of communicatlon to protect against at-
tack by the Netherlands Navy. Also, the output of the
oil fields in Netherlends Borneo is not large and could
hardly fill the needs of Japan. For these reasons, it
seems reasonable to expect that, if the Netherlands In-
dies supplies Japan with its requirements of oil, Japan
will not venture an attack on the Indies.
Mr. van Mook, the Director of the Department of
Economic Affairs, stated that he did not believe that
the Japanese would take over the Netherlands Indies oil
fialds, should the United States place an embargo on the
sale of oil to Japan, because they know very well that
the wells will be destroyed and that it will take them
two years to produce oil from the fields again.
danger

=== Page 14 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
danger or an attack een be voided, however, if the
Netherlands Indies sells oil to Japan. The Indies,
however, does not want to seli its crude petroleum --
that is all needed to supply the existing refineries,
which do a big export business in refined products.
It would mean a loss to the Netherlands Indies, there-
fore, if they were compelled to sell crude oil to Japan.
General Bakker, the Commander of the First Division
of the Netherlands Indian Arny. stated that a serious
danger existed that, if the United States placed an em-
bargo on shipments of petroleun to Japan, Janan would be-
come desperate and simply take oil wherever it could be
found. Too much pressure i iacle to induce unforesee-
able consequences. However, said the General, "the
President of the United states will robably veto the
embargo measure".
As can be observed from the above, the officials
in the Netherlands Indies are distinctly afraid of the
possible repercussions upon the Indies of an embargo
placed by the United States upon sales of petroleun to
Japan. Realizing the actualities and necessities of the
situation, however, they apparently propose to supply
Japan with the oil which would in ordinary circumstances
be obtained from the United States, even though sales
of crude oil to Japan will mean a loss to them in refin-
ing and distributing profits. As the great bulk of the
petroleum produced in the Indies is the property of and
is refined and distributed by two companies, one a Roy-
al Dutoh-Shell subsidiary and one a Socony-Vacuum sub-
sidiary, it would appear to be possible that, should
the

=== Page 15 === (confidence 0.98)
-6 -
the United Stabes impose an embar o on shipments of
oil to Japan, we shall find ourselves in the anomalous
position of compelling an American-owned corporation
in the Netherlands Indies to supply Japan with some of
the oil which we ourselves deny that country.
In regard to the ability of the Netherlands Indies
to supply Japan's requirements of oil, it may be pointed
out that Japan, prior to the conflict in China, imported
approximately 2,000,000 tons of erude and refined oil
per annum. While strict rationing in Japan tends to de-
crease requiremente, war-like operations in China and
Manchuria tend to inerease them, and it is prooable that
Japan's present needs  ve not c anred appreciably from
the pre-conflict per od.
The Metherlands Indies produces
about 7,000, 000 metric tuns o: crude oil per annum, and
while all of this crude petroleum is needed to keep the
existing refineries operating at near-capacity and to
maintain the present export trade in refined products,
it can be seen that the Netherlands Indies, if willing to
forego its refining and distributing profits on a part
of its production, could easily supply Japan with its
requirements in crude oil. This does not, however, take
into consideration the fact that most Japanese refineries
are geared to operate on high-grade California crude and
that considerable alterations would be necessary before
they could refine the lower-grade Netherlands Indies
crude.
I wish to state that 1 desire that it be understood
that I am in no way opposed to an American embargo on the
sales of materials of war to Japan; to the contrary, I
cannot

=== Page 16 === (confidence 0.96)
-- 7 -
cannot see how the United tates can take eny lesser
step under the existin circumstances and I only wish
that it had been possible to take the step at an ear-
lier date. In this despateh 1 lave only endeavered to
indicate some of the complications which wila arise
from such an embaree, in  de that the Department may
be forewarned and may be prorared to take such counter-
measures as may be deered arrropriate.
Respectfully yours,
Erle R. Dickover,
econ Consul General.

894.24/781
Dear Sir:
Replying to your letter of December 19, regarding the sale by
this Company for export of gasoline blending agents:
We have not sold any blending agents such as tetra-ethyl lead,
iso-octane, neo-hexane or alkylates for export to the Union of Soviet Socialist
Republics during the year 1939 and at the present time we have no commitments
of any kind for the sale of such products in the future.
The only blending agent which we have sold for export to Japan
during the year 1939 has consisted of our sales of hydro polymer gasoline
(iso-octane) to Asano Bussan Company. These sales amount to a total of
50, 414 barrels for the year 1939. Our conmit.nents at the present time for
the year 1940 amount to approximately 4500 parrels hydro polymer gasoline
(iso-octane).
894.24
We might mention that during the year 1939 the Manufacturing
Department advise us that they used 296l barrels hydro polymer gasoline for
blending with crude oils which we sold to Asano Bussan Company in order to meet
guaranteed specifications. We have commitments for the year 1940 for approxi-
mately 1,500,O00 barrels blended crude oils and have offers out for acceptance
which would call for delivery during 1940 of approximately 438, 000 barrels
blended crude oils, which may call for the use of hydro polymer gasoline by
the Manufacturing Department in order to meet guaranteed specifications. We
are not in position to say how much hydro polymer gasoline will be used by
the Manufacturing Department in the crude oil blends in order to meet speci-
fications, although we understand that the quantity will probably be not in excess
of 1% by volume of the crude oil. We should like to make it cloar, however,
F/N
that there is no sale of hydro polymer gasoline as such involved in the sale of
these blended orudes.
Very truly/yourd,
JH. Wuttl
J. H. TUTTLE
Secretary

December 29, 1939
Congressman Emmett O'Neal
Washington, D. C.
Dear Congresaman O'Neal:
The E1ghteenth Street Baptist Church, of more than
nine hundred members, Louisville, Kentucky, hereby voices
her sympathy for China at the outrage committed against
her by Japan's undeclared war upon her.
This war, purely for selfish ends, waged by Japan
1n violatlon of the Kellogg-Briand Peace Pact and the
Nine Power Treaty, with a barbarity that scandalizee the
world, deserves to be rebuked by all right-thinking
people and all nations that condemn treachery and bru-
tal inhumanity.
It 1s to us a matter of the most serious regret that
up to this time our nation continues to furnish them more
than fifty per cent of the munitions of war.
We appeal to our President, to Secretary of State,
Cordell Hull, and to the Ccngress of the United States,
to in some lawful way intervene for the stoppage of
supplying to the heartless, soulless military clique now
at the head of Japan, the munitions of war for the des-
truction of a peaceful nation that for her own sake and
for the world's sake should continue her national life.
Boycotts are ugly things, but ugly deeds merit hard
and effective strokes; and therefore, we are for the boy-
cott of all importations from Japan. Since we buy most
largely her silk and silk stockings, let us as people
desist from all such purchases, until such time as the
nation comes to her senses.
We are happy as Chrlstians to Join with the many who
are by petitions and resolutions seeking to stop the great
wrong this nation 1s doing herself and China in supplying
these munitions of war.
Yours ever for the right against the wrong,
M. P. HUNT, Pastor
W. A. COLVIN, Clerk
Unanimously adopted at the close of the morning service,
Sunday, December 24th.

Dear Sir:
Acknowledgment is made of the receipt of your
letter dated December 19, 1939, addressed to "Cities Service Export
Company". We assume that by "Cities Service Export Company" is
meant Cities Service Export Oil Company, which, prior to June 1l,
1936, was the exporting unit of the Cities Service Oil subsidiaries.
On the last mentioned date, Cities Service Export Oil Company was
merged into the undersigned Cities Service Oil Company, a Pennsyl-
vania corporation, which since that time has carried on the export
business through its exporting department.
Your letter inquires whether we have sold for
export during the current year to Japan and the Union of Soviet
Socialist Republics such blending agents as tetra-ethyl lead, iso-
octane, neo-hexane and alkylates. We beg to advise you that we
have made no such sales.

Mr. H. J. Halle, President of the Universal O1l
894.24/789
894.24
Products Company called Mr. Green by telephone this morning
from New York. Acting upon Mr. Green's instructione, as he
was away from the office due to 1llness, I gave to Mr. Halle
the sutstance of telegram no. 26 of January 6, noon, from
Moscow. Mr. Halle stated that he was quite relieved to
learn that the employees of hie company, Rasmussen, Rodman
and Miller and their families were safe in Moscow.
Mr. Halle referred to h1s letter of January 3, 1940
in which he stated that he had been informed by Mr. Saneyoshi,
President of the Japan Gasoline Company, that Mitsui and
Company had been granted permiesion by the United States
Government to proceed with the installation of a refinery
for the production of regular gasoline, the hot clay treat-

Mr. Halle said that his company was anxious to go

refineries. He said that although the gasoline to be
produced under these plans was regular or motor gasoline,
not of the aviatton type, he ished to be sure that the
furnishing of such information would not contravene the
policy of the Government. I informed Mr. Halle that the
policy in question had been fully explained at the meeting
of the representatives of the oil companies in the Depart-
ment on December 14, 1939 and clearly stated in the Depart-
ment's press release of December 20, 1939, and that the
Department must leave it to the judgment of the interested
companies which have the necessary technical information
to decide in any given case whether a proposed transaction
would or would not contravene that policy. Mr. Halle
implied that as this was a matter involving ordinary or

My dear Mr. Green:
In answer to your letter of December 19, 1939,
Tide Water Associated Oil Company is presently not a
manufacturer of tetraethyl lead, iso-octane, neo-hexane,
or alkylates and has made no exports of such products to
Japan or the Union of Soviet Socialist Republics. The
FILED
construction of an alkylate plant is under contemplation
and we will advise you currently, as you request, of any
future sales of alkylate products.
As you are aware, this Company for the past
ten years has owned a one-nalf interest in Mitsubishi Oil
Company, Ltd., which operates a refinery at Kawasaki, Japan.
We have also sold, through Mitsubishi interests, various
grades of crude oil containing specified percentages of
aviation gasoline of guaranteed specifications, which
latter include the octane rating obtainable with the
addition of tetraethyl lead in definite quantities. While
these crude oil blends have been specifically prepared to
meet these specifications, they have not contained alkylates,
but if the alkylete plant under contemplation is constructed
we would anticipate that such product might be used in
future blends.
Yours very truly,

Dear Dr. Morrell:
Your letter in regard to new souroes and new processes
for the production of toluene and iso-octane has been studied by
my office with a great deal of interest. Your patriotim in
offering to assign the patents conoerning these processes to the
Government to avoid dissemination of the information to foreign
countries is very greatly appreeiated.
After studying this situetion, it appears that in the
case of toluene and iso-octane the War Department should not of
itaelf undertake this development because it is felt that industry
is in a much better position, both from the standpoint of finance
and fasilities, to do the work. While undoubtedly it would be
preferable to limit the knowledge of these developments to this
country, the great number of materials with which the War Depart-
ment is concerned in a war emergenoy makes it impossible for the
Government to finanee such developments. Over a period of years
It has been found a much sounder policy to leave such developments
to eamercial companies and to spread the information mong our
own manufacturers, even at the riak of foreign govermments using
such proeesses. Only in this manner can the necessery large
produetion required in war be seeured.
The Wer Department, therefore, has no objeetion to the
comercial exploitation of these processes either in this sountry
er abroad.
I wish to thank you and your eompany for your very
liberel offer and for bringing this impertant matter te my atteme
séeme I shall appresiate very much your eomtimued coeperation with
the Wer Department in this and similar problms.
sincerely yours,
(Signed) HARRY H. VOBRB

January 18, 1940.
The Hon. Harry Flood Byrd
United States Senate
Washington, D. C.
My dear Mr. Byrd:
A few weeks ago I received a form letter inviting me
to write about any political question in which I am inter-
ested.
In H. V. Kaltenborn's broadcast yesterday, he made
the statement that thousands of gallons of petroleum
products were being sold daily by two producers on the
West Coast for use of the Japanese in their fight with
China and for use of the Russians in their attack on
Finland. I am absolutely opposed to the continuation
of this trade with these two nations. If we in America
are to stand for democracy, why should we give any
encouragement to the enemies of democracy anywhere in
the world? If the American Government 1s not going to
stand for democracy, then let us make our position clear.
For my part, I believe in democracy and intend to
give all my support to every effort to further it, not
only in America but in other parts of the world. To this
end, I hope that you will do all that is possible to
cause the trade in materials of war, with Japan and Russia,
to cease.
Very sincerely yours,
SAMUEL B. CHILTON
Editor
SC:DM

Dear Senator Byrd:
I have been tremendously concerned with the accurate
reports regarding conditions in China brought back by
returned missionaries both clerical and lay.
Not only am I concerned with the tragio condition
of China, but more specifically with what we here in
America are doing to help aggravate it. The facts seem
to be that we are now furnishing Japan with 6l% of all
the war materials she uses. This is most unfortunate in
view of our nation's conoern for the distressed people of
the earth.
Therefore, I urge as a voter, and a tax payer, and as
a Christian that you do all within your power to use what-
ever means may be effeotive 1n cutting off 1mmediately
all materials sold to Japan for use in their cruel and
needless war against a non-aggressive nation.

January 9, 1940.
Division of
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
AN 1 0 1940
Mr. Buffum called on me today. He said that
felt that there was very little purpose to his call
844.24
except that he himself wanted from a personal and a
business point of view any information or advice which
we might feel in position to give him bearing on the
question whether he should return to Japan. He ex-
894.24/79
plained that he had a Japanese partnership, that recent
developments in the field of moral embargoes had tended
to affect adversely the future of his business enterprise
which had to do with gasoline fuels -- and that he wondered
8
what the future held in store. I explained that I saw no
reason why from the point of view of personal safety he
should not return to Japan; that no one could say yet what
lay in store for business; that much would depend on how
Japan proceeds; that the American Government is asking
for fair treatment for its nationals and American business
N
FILED
2
not only in Japan but throughout the world and therefore
5
11
in China as well as in Japan; that there are various method
by which the United States can, if necessary, bring pressures
F
to bear upon Japan; and that if Japanese authorities continue
N
to abuse American nationals and American interests, the
likelihood

=== Page 78 === (confidence 0.98)
-2 -
likelihood is that some and perhaps several pressures
will as the situation develops be applied. There followed
some discussion of the trade situation. Mr. Buffum gave
me the impression of being reasonably well informed,
sympathetic toward this Government's objectives, quick to
grasp the purport of general observations, and not dis-
turbed by the indication which I gave him of a trend.
He thanked me for the time I gave him and I assured him
that it had been a pleasure to me to talk the situation
over with him.
Stanley K.'Hornbeck

policies. It considers American support to Japanese

aggression in China one of the more shameful episodes
DR. EDWARD HUGHES PRUDEN
MRS. GEORGE ROSE
in the history of our international relations, and
DR. CHARLES EDWARD RUSSELL
DR. JOHN W. RUSTIN
HON. BYRON N. SCOTT
has throughout the past two years done whatever it
DR. DANIEL N. SHOEMAKER
MR. ARTHUR CLARENDON SMITH
could to arouse in Americans a sense af responsibil-
FED1-1940
Hon. HEnrY G. TEIGAN
MRS. BENJAMIN WALLACE
ity for and antagonism against a condition of affairs
FTLED
DR. CHARLES WESLEY
under which the United States is a partner in the con-
quest of a friendly nation and a potentially vast and
cooperative customer for American exports.
In your famous "quarantine the aggressors"
speech of more than two years ago, you obviously shared
the philosophy which lies behind this point of view.
F/N
Then, however, your thinking was far in advance of that
711.942
of the population of the United States as a whole. In
the intervening two years, partly, no doubt, as a result

=== Page 87 === (confidence 0.99)
[The President--2]
of the campaign of education carriad on by the American Committee
for Non-Participation in Japanese Aggression, the Washington Com-
mittee for Aid to China, and other similar groups, American opin-
ion in the matter has changed. It is now safe to say that the
American people, both as individual American citizens and as
represented in the Congress, are ready to follow a leadership
which promises to stop American participation in at least one of
the major acts of international aggression.
The initiative, consequently, now rests with the Execu-
tive. We respectfully suggest, therefore, that the President of
the United States and the Secretary of State consider carefully
the possibility of assuming leadership which will result in:
1) An official delaration that the United States
will adhere to the policy of not recognizing changes in
China's territorial and administrative integrity, such
as the proposed Chinese Central Government under Wang
Ching-wei, brought about by force.
2) Postponement of consideratian of a new Commer-
cial Treaty with Japan until there is a responsible gov-
ernment in Japan both willing and able to maintain treaty
obligations.
3) Discontimuation of the shipment of American ma-
terials for war purposes to Japan by means of a "moral
embargo", by leglslative enactment, or in any other way
appropriate to the purpose.
4) A major loan to the Chinese Industrial Coopera-
tives in order to enable them to purchase in America
goods equivalent to those now exported to Japan and thus
to mitigate the inconvenience to American exporters which
might otherwise arise.
It is the belief of the Washington Committee for Aid to
China that the restoration of American integrity in international

=== Page 88 === (confidence 0.99)
[The President--3]
relations which the accomplishment of these ends would exhibit
would go far in the direction of international peace. Certain-
ly American protestations of horror at aggression in Europe
are ineffective so long as they are combined with participation
in the most vicious of all aggressions in Asia. Finally, it
must be understood that any American action or inaction which
has the effect of supporting the hands of the irresponsible
Japanese militarists is an absolute dis-service to the Japanese
people as a whole and a complete reversal of our historical
American tradition of supporting liberal ism and democracy wher-
ever it exists throughout the world.
Very respectfully yours,
MORTIMER GRAVES, Chairman
Washington Committee for Aid to China

I have just learned that Associated Ethyl Company, Ltd
94.24/805
British corporation which controls the rights to the ethyl process
(oxcept for the North American continent and adjacent islands) is nego-
tiating concerning the erection of a plant in Manchukuo. Apparently
they have no intention of finalizing any agreement at this time; but,
as President of the American holding company, The Forak Company, which
represents the American shareholders' interest in Associated Ethyl, I
have advised Associated Ethyl that, in our opinion, the moral embargo
applies to this project, and that we cannot favor or assist it.

seems to be directly at variance with the policy which that government expressed
when consulted last September on a similar questien by the Shell Company in

in Japanese-controlled territory.

and helpful to everyone concerned if Associated Ethyl were to advise the
Japanese interest that it is ready to continue with the discussions of the
and of the very large American shareholding interest in Associated Ethyt.
no agreement can actually be completed until that embargo is lifted.
Would you approve of this course?

of the British government to take a definite position on this matter, the
suggested course might be an acceptable compremise which would mot unduly
penalise the British interests in Associated Ethyl, which are very large.

SIR:
I have the honor to refer to uespatch no. 542,
dated Jamuary 15, 1940, from the American Consul at
894.24/788
Matamoros, a copy of which has been received by
this Consulate, concerning the loading of a quantity
of lead bars, said to total in weight some 2,000
tons, on the Japanese Motorship AKAGI MARU, of the
E
Nippon Yusen Kaisha Line, on January 14, 1940, the
3
FTLED
一
lead bars being marked "ASARCO" (American Smelting
31940
and Refining Company) and "MEXICO", also that
additional stocks of lead bars c med by the Ameri-
can Smelting and Refining Company are now in the
port warehouses, and that a large pile of scrap
iron and steel which may be the property of deal-
ers in New York and possibly not for shipment,
F/N
therefore, to Japan, was observed near the docks.
Two American companies, with vast mining
interests

=== Page 100 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
interests in Mexico, have metal refineries in
Monterrey, one also operating a smelter here. The
company with both smelter and refinery is Compañia
Metalúrgica Peñoles, S. A., subsidiary of the
American Metal Company, Limited, 61 Broadway, New
York City. The other is the American Smelting &
Refining Company, whose head office in Mexico is
at Mexico City, and in the United States the
Equitable Building, New York City.
During the calendar year of 1939 the produc-
tion of these two refineries of refined lead
amounted to 285,519,126 pounds, valued at $8,425,086.
of this amount, only 2,379,045 pounds, valued at
$122,838, was exported to the United States for
consumption in that country as covered by consular
invoices certified at this Consulate. This was
only a small part of the production as the lead
normally finds its principal markets in European
countries and Japan.
Since the outbreak of war in Europe, there
has been difficulty encountered in marketing lead,
and new markets had to be found for shipments
which normally would have gone to European countries.
It is understood that during the past three months
most of the production has been shipped to Japan,
Scandinavian countries, South American countries,
South Africa, and Russia. This information was
reported to the Department in this Consulate's
Annual Economic Review for the calendar year of
1939 and in several of the regular monthly reports
submitted

=== Page 101 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
submitted on political and economic conditions in
this district.
So far this month nine invoices have been
certified at this Consulate covering shipments of
lead to the United States, all by Compañ1a Metalúr-
gica Peñoles, S. A. One, certified on January 10,
1940, covering 448,031 pounds, valued at $22,402,
was consigned to its head office in New York City,
while eight were certified on January 25, 1940,
covering a total of 2,239,886 pounds, valued at
$89,595, which were consigned to Phelps Dodge
Refining Corporation at Corpus Christi, Texas. In-
quiry made today of the local company brought the
information that the lead covered by the eight
invoices referred to were sent to Corpus Christi,
Texas, in July and August last year, and held in
storage for subsequent shipment to a foreign country,
but as the consignee decided to transfer the entire
amount to another location farther north for con-
sumption in the United States, it was necessary to
have consular invoices certified covering the eight
shipments.
The lead loaded on the Japanese vessel at
Brownsville undoubtedly was refined by the American
Smelting & Refining Company in Monterrey. Its
office, upon inquiry, stated that no shipments
have been made to Brownsville, Texas, for at least
two months, but prior to that considerable lead was
sent there where it was stored by a broker for ship-
ment abroad.
A11

=== Page 102 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-

the two Monterrey refining companies are made by
their respective head offices in New York City
and little information is divulged by the local
offices.
With reference to the scrap iron and steel
stored at Brownsville, this Consulate has no infor-
mation as to its origin.

(1)
officers of the Department. with respect to Mr. Geller's
894.2
request that a conference of the interested parties be
held to discuss ways of relieving the difficulties of the
Japan Gasoline Company and the Universal Oil Products Company,
it is my opinion that such a conference is entirely unnecessary
8
At the meeting of the representatives of the oil and
engineering companies in the Department on December 14, 1939,
all those present exoressed their willingness to cooperate
with the Government in any policy which the Government might
formulate. During the discussion at this meeting, it was
FILED
brought out that the Universal Oil Products Company and
2.
13
several other companies already had contracts with the Jap-
1
anese for the construction of plants for the production of
aviation gasoline or the delivery of technical information,
and furthermore some companies were then negotiating for
new contraots of this nature. In order that the policy to
F/N
be formulated should apply equally to all, and to the end
that

=== Page 121 === (confidence 0.95)
-2-
that that no company should be given a competitive advantage,
it was decided among the interested parties that all exist-
ing contracts, and negotiations for future contracts, should
be suapended. The Universal Oil Products Company was among
those which agreed to abide by this agreement.
Consequently no exception cen be made to the application
of the Government's policy. The grenting of any exception
would not only constitute a violation of policy, but it would
break down the entire structure upon which the cooperation of
the business intereets has been built. Mr. Celler should be
informed that no excention can be made in any case.

=== Page 123 === (confidence 0.99)

Mr. Celler said that he fully understood the position
of the Department; that he had no interest in the matter
except an interest which had developed as & result of his
friendehip with Mr. Cohen, a former member of Congress;
that he had heard nothing further from Mr. Cohen in regard
to the matter since his conversation with Mr. Ballantine;
but that, if he should hear anything further from Mr. Cohen,
he intended to suggest to Mr. Cohen that the two of them
call on me to discuss the matter further.
Shortly after my conversation with Mr. Celler, Mr.
Halle, President of the Universal Oil Produots Company,
called me by telephone from New York. He said that
Mr. Celler and Mr. Cohen and three Japanese representa-
tives of the Japan Gasoline Company had called on him
the othe r day and at great length had attempted to per-
suade him to insist to the Department that the Department
must authorize an exception to the policy referred to
above in respect to the contract between the Univereal
O1l Products Company and the Japan Gasoline Company. He
said that he had stated categorically that his company
intended to adhere fully to the policy of the Government
and

=== Page 124 === (confidence 0.99)
-3
and that he would not under any circumstances request the
Department to make any exception to that policy. He said
that Mr. Celler had thereupon invited him to dine with
him and that he had refused the invitation, saying that
he did not want to do anything which might even have the
appearance of an attempt to deal with these matters except
in direct conversation with officials of the Department of
State with whom he had already had conversations. Mr. Celler
then urged him to dine with him the next time he was in
Washington. He said that he refused that invitation also,
telling Mr. Celler that he would not even see him except
in the office of the Universal Oil Products Company in
New York.

The Honorable

I have the honor to enclose a copy of a letter dated
January 19, 1940, from the Reverend William A. McCurdy,
an American missionary resident in Chungking, and of the
petition enclosed therewith, both relating to the desire
of twenty odd American citizens that the United States
place an embargo on the shipment of munitions of war to
Japen.

=== Page 126 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
2/
Japan. There is enclosed, also, a copy of my reply to
Mr. McCurdy of January 19, 1940.
It will be noted that I refused to transmit the
petition to the Department of State, as asked by its
signers, on the ground that it was to be inferred that
not only would there be publication of the petition, out
also of the fact that it had been transmitted through
official agencies, and that in view of the probability
that the document, as drafted, would work for ill-feeling
between the people of the United States and the people of
China, the Embassy was u willin to assume responsibility
for apparent connection with such publication.
The petition contains a bald assertion that the
United States for two years and a half has "participated
in the Japanese aggression in China by continuing to
facilitate the supplying of the sinews of war".
I did
not, in my reply, deny this assertion, but I objected to
the fact that it was not offset by counter-balancing state-
ments regarding other phases of the attitude taken by the
United States in regard to current events in this area.
The Chinese naturally feel great resentment at the assist-
ance in prosecuting the hostilities that Japan has been
able to obtain from the United States, and tney regard
this as inconsistent with official declarations made by
the United States in condemnation of the Japanese invasion
and in support of the Chinese position. The fact is, how-
ever, that the United States has taken a stand in opposition
to Japan's invasion of China more decided than that of any
other nation except that of China, itself. I felt that an
unrelieved aocusation of American "participation" in the
hostilities, unaccompanied by any reference to American
opposition

=== Page 127 === (confidence 0.97)
- 3 -
opposition thereto, was calculated to convey a very in-
accurate impression of the American attitude and would be
extremely apt to arouse animosity against the United
States. Moreover, a statement of this sort coming from
American citizens would be a valuable weapon in the hands
of any person, Jananese or Chinese, who might wish to
incite Chinese sentiment against American citizens.
I have heard nothing further in regard to this petition.
I trust that the Department will approve of the action taken
by the Embassy in this matter.
Respectfully yours,
For the Ambassador:
Willgo n. leeh
Willys R. Peck,
Counselor of Embassy.

=== Page 131 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No.2
to Despatch No.432 Dated
(COPY)
January 22, 1940.
Chungking, January 19, 1940.
Reverend William A. McCurdy,
American Methodist Episcopal Church,
Chungking.
Sir:
I have carefully read your letter of January 19,
1940, and its enclosure, an appeal signed by more than
twenty persons said to be American citizens and address-
ed to the President, the Congress, and the People of the
United States of America.
From your letter I infer that it is the intention
not only to publish this appeal, but also a statement that
it has been transmitted to the United States through Amer-
ican Government agencies. If that inference is correct, I
cannot accede to your request that the Embassy transmit the
message.
The document as it stands refers to one phase, only, of
the attitude taken by the United States toward current events
in the Far East and the Embassy is unwilling to assume re-
sponsibility for apparent connection with its publication in
a foreign country.
. It is the desire of the Embassy to pro-
mote friendship between the people of China and the people
of the United States, and it is unable to believe that such
a partial and unrelieved statement is calculated to achieve
that effect.
The statement is, therefore, returned to you for such
disposition as its signers wish to make of it.
Yours truly,
For the Ambassador,
Willys R. Po

=== Page 136 === (confidence 0.99)
Portlandville, New York
Sunday, January 28, 1940
Honor Cordell Hull,
Secretary of State,
Unites States Government,
Washington, D,C.
Dear Sir:
We, the members and friends of uhe Porulandville Meuhodist
Church desire to take this opportunity to express our condemnation
of Japanese militarists who since July 7, 1937 have been waging a
campaign of terrible brutality against the civilian population of
China.
The sack of Nanking is indicative of the fact that the invaders
of China have adopted a policy of deliberate savagery and cruelty. In
defenseless Chinese cilies tons of bombs, explosive nd incendiary,
have been dropped by Japanese bomhers upon multitudes of men, women
and children in narrow streets and crowded dwellings. Low-flying air-
planes in utterly inhuman fashion have machine-gunned streams of de-
fenseless and fear stricken humon heings.
Wherever the invading Japanese armies have gone there has been an
unceasing campaign of slaurhter, destruction and violence that has few
parallels in history. rowns and villages have been burned, food has been
wantonly destroyed, young girls and women have been attacked in every
conceivable manner and mass executions have been carried out with re-
volting barbarity.
"In 193,, the Unites States provided 54% of Japan's imports of
essential war materials. In l93o, this proportion rose to 56%. During
the first eight months of 1939, it rose still higher. And since the
outbreak of war in Europe and the cutting off of practically all E-
uropean sources of supply, America is supplying Japan about oO% of the
materials imported to keep her war machine going forward in China."
To a large extent, therefore, we are responsible for the destruction,
suffering and sorrow that has taken place in China. We cannot be held
guiltless. Now that the 19ll Trade Treaty has been abrogated as of Jan-
uary 26, 1940 - we should make amends for what we have done to our
brothers and sisters across the Pacific. Ne call atoantion to the fact
that many congressional leaders -- in both houses, from both parties,
and from all sections of the country---have vigorously denounced Japan's
course in China, and have proposed that an embargo on war malerials be
imposed, or that the President be empowered to impose such an embargo
on war materials.
We no longer desire to be as we have been - the chief armoror of
the Japanese armies of aggression. we, therefore, urge the Secretary of
State to do all in his power to have Congress take a stand for non-
participation in Japanese aggression. With this end in view we urge the-
passage of immediate legislation designed to stop the shipment of basio
war materials and supplies to one which obviously is the aggressor
nation.
Signed:

Congressman Hamilton Fish telephoned and said he
894.659
had had a number of debates with Upton Close on the
question whether Japan had factories and refineries for
the manufaoture of high-octane gas. Mr. Fish said that
he would like to get from us authoritative information
inrregard to that question. I replied that it was my
understanding that Japan purchased from the United
States considerable quantities of high-octane gas but
FEB 8=1940
FILED
that I oould not give Mr. Fish definitive information
on the question whether Japan had factories or refin-
eries whioh produoed high-octane gas. I said that it
seemed to me that the real question involved was whether
or not Japan had the technioal processes which were
needed for the manufacture of high-ootane gas.

question in which he was interested. I said that we
would be glad to look into the matter and make inquiries
with a view to communicating with him further.

SUBJECT:
Question whether Japan manufactures high-ootane gas.
DCR

1-1403
I telephoned Congressman Hamilton Fish and said that
in the absence of Mr. Hamilton I wished to give Mr. Fish
certain information which Mr. Hamilton promised on January 1l
to endeavor to obtain for him on the subject of whether
Japan manufactures high-octane gas. My statement was as
follows:
Information in regard to the production in Japan of
high-test aviation gasoline has been regarded as a military
secret for many years and for that reason it has been
8=1940
Statistios in regard to the produotion in Japan and the
importation into Japan even of ordinary gasoline and other
ordinary items, such as pig iron, automobiles, and so forth,
have not been published sinoe July l937. The "law for the
protection

=== Page 146 === (confidence 0.98)
一2-
protection of secrets of resourses for military use"
provides severe penalties for the dissemination of informa-
tion in regard to the amount of production of and capacity
of production for important commodities used for military
purposes. Aocording to the limited information available
in this Department, as well as that available in the Commerce
and Navy Departments, Japan has a certain number of plants
equipped to produce blending agents for the transformation
of ordinary gasoline into high-quality gasoline. These
plants, however, are few in number and are entirely
insufficient to make possible the production in Japan of
more than a small part of the high-quality aviation gasoline
necessary for Japanese military, naval and civil aviation.
The blending agents produced in these plants and the methods
mployed in the production of these blending agents are, for
the most part, outmoded in comparison with the agents and
methods now employed in this country and are not adapted
to the production of the highest quality aviation gasoline
whioh our own Army and Navy use exclusively. Statistios
of United States exports of aviation gasoline have been
shown separately only during l939. According to the
Department of Commerce there have been exported from the
United States to Japan during the first eleven months of
1939 513,411 barrels (of 42 gallons eaoh) of aviation
gasoline of 80-l00 octane number. During the same period
there

=== Page 147 === (confidence 0.97)
- 3-
there were exported to Kwantung 65,937 barrels and to
China 113,000 barrels.
Mr. Fish said that all he wanted to know was that
Japan had plants equipped to produce high-quality gasoline.
He saw no reason why, therefore, Japan could not extend
those plants and become self-sufficient in high-test
gasoline or why Japan could not obtain from Germany or
Russia all the information Japan desired in regard to
teohnical processes. I said that I did not know whether
Germany and Russia had the teohnical processes and that
it seemed to me that the figures I had given him for
exports from the United States to Japan of aviation gasoline
indicated that Japan was not in a position to supply herself
from domestic plants.
Mr. Fish thanked me for my information.
211
FE:BalVantine:REK

We have under consideration the acceptance
of a contract for Nickel, Chrome Molybdenum Steel
Turbine and Pinion Shafts for export to Japan and in
view of the recent termination of our trade treaty
with that country we, naturally, wish to deliberate
upon our status as a manufacturer until the comple-
tion of the work and payment has been made.
We understand that our present relations
with Japan are on a day to day basis and do not
assume that any serious difficulties will arise in
the future, however, we are interested in knowing,
if, in the event that an embargo is deemed necessary,
whether shafting such as mentioned above is likely to
be included and, if so, would it be permissible for the
manufacturer to complete and ship such material on
contracts that were entered into prior to the enact-
B1

ment of such embargo and under the circumstance that
payment for the material would be made against rail-
31940
road bills of lading and invoices.
We shall be very glad to know if the State
Department has a fixed policy in this respect and to
receive any information that would be of assistance
to us in our present deliberations upon the matter.
Very truly yours,
CAMDEN PORGE COMPANY.

Judge Covington or Govington, Burling, Rublee,
894.24/824
Acheson, and Shorb, Washington attorneys, counsel for
the Kennecott Copper Company, called at my office this
morning accompanied by Mr. Carl Ulrich of the company.
They explained that the Braden Copper Company, the
Chilean subsidiary of the Kennecott Copper Company,
had received an order from Japanese interests for 75
tons of molybdenum and that the company felt that it
must authorize its subsidiary to accept the order. They
were well aware that the exportation of molybdenum from
the United States to countries the armed forces of whioh
are engaged in the unprovoked bombing of civilian popu-
lations from the air was contrary to the expressed polioy
of this Government. They were also aware of the fact
that exportation from Chile, even pursuant to sale by an
E
F
American-owmed company, would not contravene the polioy
8
of
1940

=== Page 156 === (confidence 0.96)
-2-
of this Government as expressed. Nevertheless, they felt
that they should explain why the Kennecott Copper Company
felt obliged to authorize its subsidiary to accept this
order. They said that, if it were to refuse to accept the
order, they feared that the Japanese might complain to the
Chilean Government and that the Chilean Government would
intervene and force the company to make the sale. In fact,
this might be an opening wedge for control of exportation
by the Chilean Government.
In answer to my inquiries, Mr. Ulrich stated that the
Braden Copper Company had recently begun to produce molyb-
denum in the form M0s-2 at the rate of between 70 and 90 tons
per month as a by-product of its main operations.
It should be noted that, even if the Japanese were to
purchase all of the molybdenum produced by the Braden company,
the quantity obtainable from that source would not amount
per annum to more than approximately one-sixth of the quan-
tity which Japan has been purchasing per annum from the
United States.
Joseph C. Green

Dear Sir:
This Company has received an offer from Mitsui and
844.24
Company of Tokyo, Japan, for the purchase of the equipment,
specified in the attached list, which Mitsui informs us it
wishes to use for the manufacture of methanol.
894.24/829
About ten years ago this Company shut down a plant
which had been built for the manufacture of methanol and
since that time such equipment as was not required within
the Company has been offered for sale at various times for
use in any manufacture requiring gas at high pressure, as
for example, the manufacture of ammonia and the manufacture
of methanol. Attached is copy of a memorandum which explains
the situation more in detail.
6
The second-hand equipment which we contemplate
selling at substantially original cost is special only to
the extent that it can be used for the compression and
handling of gas at high pressure. Equipment of this charac-
ter can be obtained as standard equipment from manufacturing
companies in many countries, but probably not for immediate
delivery.
The sale will cover only the items specified and
will include no technical knowledge or information whatever.
We can only assume the purchase is made by Mitsui and Company
in order to save time in the construction of a plant which
would be of their own design, or in any event without any
assistance from this Company. Before concluding this trans-
action we desire to place the subject before your Department
for consideration. We respectfully request that we be advised
3
FILED
whether or not you have any objection to the contemplated sale
N
at your early convenience.

=== Page 173 === (confidence 0.99)
CQPI
PONT dO NEMOURS & COMPANY
Incorporated
Wilmington, Delaware
IONIA DEPARTMENT
January 31, 1940
MR. E. A. RYKENBOER
R. & H. CHEMICALS DEPT.
Japan has, for many years, been an importer of
methanol, the largest use for which was undoubtedly in the manu-
facture of plastics. While several years ago one or more small
methanol plants were built in Japan, it was always necessary to
import a considerable percentage of the methanol requirements.
Part of this methanol came from the United States, but most of
it from Germany. These imports were in addition to Japan's
manufacture, it having been reported that several methanol
plants were under construction.
During 1939 exports of methanol from the United
States to Japan have been considerably larger than heretofore.
In the latter part of the year the reason, which can be most
readily presumed, is that imports from Germany were entirely
cut off. Before the start of the European War it is quite pos-
sible that Germany was imposing restrictions upon exporting
methanol. No figures are available on Japanese consumption,
manufacture and imports to form any picture of the methanol
situation in Japan.
It might be presumed that some of this methanol
was being used as a motor fuel ingredient, particularly for
aeroplanes. Methanol, in conjunction with gasoline and a
solubiliser to make the methanol stay in solution with gasoline
has certain anti-knock properties. However, at the price which
the Japanese have paid for methanol it is difficult to see how
this use of methanol for motor fuel could be attractive in
competition with gasoline, which they have been free to import.
The most intelligent guess, therefore, that can be made is that
the imports of methanol have been for plastics manufacture.
About 10 years ago this Company shut down a plant
which had been built for the manufacture of methanol. This plant
comprised gas production facilities, compressors, synthesis
equipment, etc. Such equipment as was not required within the

=== Page 174 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
Company has been offered for sale at various times for use in any
manufacture requiring gas at high pressure, as, for example, the
manufacture of ammonia and the manufacture of methanol. Among
the ones to whom this equipment has been offered have been companies
in Japan. No real interest was expressed by them in this equipment
until recently, when they have expressed the desire to purchase
the equipment for the manufacture of methanol. This Company has
taken the position that it has no interest in assisting any company
in Japan in the manufacture of methanol by giving them any informa-
tion whatsoever on the process. However, the second-hand equipment
at the plant, which has been shut down, which equipment is special
only to the extent that it can be used for the compression to and
handling of gas at high pressure, is available for sale. High
pressure compressors can be obtained as standard equipment from many
countries and from compressor manufacturing companies in this
country. The interest now expressed by the Japanese in securing
this particular second-hand equipment must be that they could
save time in the construction of a plant, which would be of their
own design, or at least without any assistance from this Company.
(s) F. A. Wardenburg
F. A. WARDENBURG, GENERAL MANAGER
FAW.ejm

The Honorable Allen T. Treadway

Washington, D. C.
My dear Mr. Treadway:
There have been many statements in the
newspapers to tne effect that Russian and Japanese
bombing of civilians have been possible only through
supplies of American aviation gusoline. If it is a
fact tnat American refined gasoline is being furnished
to those two countries the Government records will
doubtless snow what producers and refiners are furnish-
ing it.
While it may be that the Government is not in
a position to impose an official embargo on the expor-
tation of gasoline, I believe that the American people
can make it more profitable for such producers and
refiners, if it is known who they are, to discontinue
such exportation.
I shoula like very much to know who these
producers and refiners are, and I believe tnat tne
public would also like to have tnis information. I
would suggest that it be published. If, for some
reason, wnich I do not understand, tnis would not be
feasible, I shall be obliged if you can nave tnis
information furnished me.
Thanking you for your interest -
Sincerely yours,
R. H. OLIPHANT
255 Pomeroy Avenue

Honorable Cordell Hull,

State Department,

My steemed Sir,-
894.24/837
To avoid foreign entangle-
ments and keep this country out of war I
am convinced that the best way to do so is
to refuse to ship them war materials or
such equipment as may be used for war purposes.
894.24
I understand that there is now a proposal
before the Congress to place an embargo on
such shipments to Japan. I would not favor
a proposition to prohibit such shipments to
Japan and permit them to be made to others.
But in my opinion refusing to allow such
shipments in this instance is a beginning
to show our attitude.
Asking your pardon for this rather
unwarranted intrusion I·am,

up with public matters, but I would certainly like to have you adrise
me just what kind of trade we are carrying on with Japan since anuary
26th of last year.Is any scrap iron,or other war material being shipped
Japan?
The economic conditions in Japan  serious,and if we will bring economic
pressure to bear,she will fail in her conquest of China.I might say it
is not necessary to issue a general embargo against shipments moving to
Japan.We can hold scrap iron,and other war supplies in this country as
necessary,due to world conditions,and can so advise Japan.
Japan deserves no sympathy. She has been engaged in a horrible war
to take that which does nct belong to her.The one possible chance to put
n end to wars of conguest,is to give no help whatever to nations wnich
rwilling to engage in such an undertaking.
I trust our government will withdraw any support now being given Japan,
as I think it would be a calamity for Japan to succeed in her undertaking
With best wishes, I am,
Cordially yours

1—-1493
During the course of a call made at his request,
894.24
Mr. Morishima, Counselor of the Japanese Embassy, referred
again to the contract between the Universal Oil Products
Company and the Japan Gasoline Company which had been
7:1942/529
mentioned in the Japanese Embassy's note of January 6 and
in this Government's reply on the subject of moral embargoes.
MA
Mr. Morishime had also brought up this matter in his
conversation with me on January 30 and the Japanese Ambassad
ED
had brought up the matter with the Secretary on January 31.
9
Mr. Morishima asked that we give special consideration
to the question of this particular contract. He said that the
Japan Gasoline Company had contracts with three other
Japanese companies; that the Japan Gasoline Company found
itself, as a result of the policy of this Government in
discouraging

=== Page 225 === (confidence 0.97)
121662
- 2 -
discouraging the sale of technical processes for the
production of high-test gasoline, in a very difficult
financial situation; and that the Japan Gasoline Company
would be satisfied temporarily with partial fulfillment
of its contract with the Universal Oil Products Company.
I told Mr. Morishima that I would of course make
record of what he had said but that I would ask him to refer
to what the Secretary had said to the Japanese Ambassador on
January 3l. I said that I could offer him no encouragement
whatsoever that favorable considerable could be given to
his request. I said that the American Government had adopted
a general policy which was clearly expleined in this Govern-
ment's reply to the Japanese Embassy's note of January 6;
and that where someone had requested that an exception be
made in a particular case the decision had uniformly been
that it was not feasible to endeavor to make exceptions.
Mr. Morishima kept pressing the matter and I reiterated
that while I would of course make record of what he had
said I could offer no encouragement in the matter.
32

894.24/846
Mr. Acker and Mr. Harmon of the Baldwin Locomotive
Works called this afternoon to state that they had received
an order for delivery to Japan of a press to be used in the
manufacture of armor plate for naval vessels. They said
that the proposed contract would provide for work on the
oart of the Baldwin Company to a value of about $1,00o,000,
but assured me that there would be no extension of credit
and that one-third payment would be made on the signature
of the contract, one-third when the contract was half com-
pleted and one-third upon final delivery in this country.
They inquired whether or not this proposed contract would
conflict with any law, regulation, or policy of this Govern-
ment.
I replied that neither the Neutrality Act nor any oth
O
law prohibited the export of material of this sort to ny
foreign country, that the so-called "moral embargo" did not
apply to presses for the manufacture of armor plate and
F/N
that the question of whether or not the contract should be
concluded

=== Page 228 === (confidence 0.91)
concluded would therefore appear to be one solely for
the determination of the Baldwin Comoany itself.
Charles W. Yost

U. 3. sales of war materials to Japan during the first eight months of 1939
(Jamuary through August) totaled $96,577,896. This compares with $97,292,781 for the
comparable period in 1938, a slight decrease of $714,885 or 0.73 per cent; and with
$115,944,996 for the comparable 1937 per1od, a drop of $19,367,100 or 16.70 per cent
(see Table 1). However, during the same eight months of 1939 the value of all exports
from the United States to Japan showed a far sharper decline from the comparable
figures for 1938 and 1937, the reductions being 10.79 per cent and 37.29 per cent
(see Table 2-A).
If U. S. exports to Japan are grouped into three major categories, as in
Tables 2-A and 2-B, it is seen that, whereas in the first eight months of this year
war material sales was off only 0.73 per cent in value, compared with the correspond-
ing months of l938, the value of raw cotton exports recorded a drastic decline of
48.33 per cent while the value of exports other than war materials and raw cotton fell
by 39.30 per cent. A comparison of dollar figures for the January-August, 1939,
period with those for the like interval in 1937 shows that, as against a drop of 16.70
per cent in war material sales, raw cotton shipments went down 60.92 per cent and ex-
ports other than war materials and raw cotton dropped 6l.4l per cent.
The significant fact is that the ratio of war material exports to all ex-
ports from the United States to Japan for the January-August period of the three years
has continued to rise until it reached 71.28 per cent in 1939, as compared with 64.06
per cent in 1938 and 53.66 per cent in 1937 (see Table 2-B). In other words, of every

materials sold by the United States to Japan during the eight months of 1939, as com-

nonferrous metals, leather, zinc, and arms and ammunition.

5.42 per cent ($1,676,744) less than the figure for the first eight months of 1938, it
represents a gain of 16.08 per cent ($1,006, 306) over the corresponding figure for 1937.

on examining the sub-iteme, sales of gasolone and other petroleum motor fuel in con-
tainers are seen to have risen from only $42, 141 for the eight months in 1937 to
$1,882,004 for the corresponding 1938 period, and then reached a new high of $3, 221,074
in the same months of 1939 (refer to Table 1). This is of special significance because
the overwhelming proportion of this sub-item - 85.84 per cent of the value and 87.64
Iron and steel scrap, the second largest major item, took 19.97 per cent,
practically one-fifth of the aggregate total value of all war materials exported from
this country to Japan during the eight months of this year. Shipments totaling
$19,290,297 represent a gain of $14,533,550 or 32.73 per cent over the corresponding
1938 figure; and the quantity sold increased even more than the value, from 881,102
gross tons to 1, 292,924 gross tons, or a gain of 46.74 per cent. In relation to the
comparable l937 level - which, be it noted, was most abnormally high due to rush orders
by Japan in preparation for the China adventure - sales decreased 46.46 per cent in
value, but only 25.85 per cent in physical volume. The marked rise in shipments for
the Jamuary-August period this year compared with exportations for last year's period
shows Japan's increasing difficulty in obtaining iron and steel scrap elsewhere, thus
rendering it absolutely necessary for that country to make practically all of her pur-
chases in the American market. It will be recalled that already in 1938 the United
States share in total world exports of iron and steel scrap to Japan was 90.39 per
cent.2
There can be no doubt that the peroentage share is even higher this year.
The heavy buying of scrap iron and steel by Japan, particularly on the
Pacific coast, has been a potent factor in the sharp rise of prices for the commodity.
1/ The percentage proportions are based on the U. S. Commerce Department data for the

=== Page 241 === (confidence 0.99)
1 iron and steel producers are finding themselves confronted with difficulties in
a rising price situation, since about one-half of the metal used for charging open
hearth furnaces is scrap. These producers fear that any further price increase will
render profitable operation impossible. Already there is agitation for measures by
Congress to restrict exports of scrap iron and steel.
Apropos to Japan's iron procurement problem, attention ought to be called to
the increased shippings of iron ore from the United States. These, while not large in
value, at once indicate the existence of an acute iron famine in Japan and the countrys
inability to obtain an adequate supply from the usual nearby sources to meet the
mounting wartime demand. U. S. official figures for iron ore exports to Japan rose
from 290 tons for the whole year of 1938 - which compared with only 50 tons in 1937 - to
9,612 tons for the first eight months of 1939, with a velue of $140, 654.
Rising to the forefront in the 1938 period as the third most important item,
metal-working machinery has further increased its share in total U. S. war-material
exports to Japan from 15.45 per cent to 18.40 per cent during the eight months of this
year, as compared with its share of only 4.87 per cent in the corresponding months of
1937. For the eight months under consideration sales have risen from $5,654,799 in 1937
to $15,032,300 in 1938 and further to $17,774,691 this year.
It is hardly possible to emphasize too strongly the great significance of
the increasingly heavy volume of metal-working machinery supplied by the United States
to Japan. For the latter suffers not only from poverty of raw material resources that
are of vital importance alike in wartime and in time of peace, but to this day it re-
mains the most backward among the leading world powers from the standpoint of machine
tecinique and national productive power. This relatively weak industrial potential
sets a serious liit to Japan's munition-making capacity and ability to wage modern
warfare. Tremendous efforts have been made by Japan to overcome this handicap through
an ambitious program of developing its engineering and heavy industries. American
maohine tools are obviously playing a most vital role in the equipping of new plants
for war produotion.
- 5 -

=== Page 242 === (confidence 0.99)
In 1938, the United States furnished 67.09 per cent of the metal-working
to jump upward, particularly with the elimination of Germany as a major source of
supply because of the European conflict. The obstacles to Japan's maximum purchasing
program in this country in regard to machine tools have been the shortage of foreign
exchange and the unwillingness on the part of American exporters to do business on a
credit basis. However, it is to be wondered whether the attitude of American business
has not undergone some change in view of the fact that recently the United Engineering
and Foundry Company transacted a major credit deal, in the form of investment in the
Shibaura Engineering Works, of some $5,000,000 of rolling mill equipment from its
Wooster, Ohio plant (see American Machinist, August 9, 1939, p. 612-E).
Copper, the fourth largest major item, has also increased its share of the
total value of United States war-material exports to Japan from ll.ll per cent and
10.94 per cent, respectively, for the first eight months of 1937 and 19:38 to 15.97 per
cent for the same months of 1939. Sales during the 1939 period amounted to $15, 426, 411,
a gain of $4,78l,189 or 44.91 per cent over the corresponding figure for 1938, and a
gain of $2,543,742 or 19.75 per cent against the comparable level of 1937.
The above four items of dominant importance - petroleum and products, iron
and steel scrap, metal-working machinery, and copper - together comprised 84.63 per
cent ($81,745,906) of the total $96,577,896 worth of strategic war necessities sold to
Japan during the January-August period of this year, compared with 73.12 per cent and
68.80 per cent, respectively, of the corresponding totals for 1938 and 1937 (see
cumulative per cent columns of Table 3).
Turning to the other major items, we see that a continuous and most pre-
cipitous deoline took place in iron and steel semi-manufactures other than iron and
steel scrap, with sales shrinking to only $1,968, 245 during the eight months of this
year from $22,922,136 in the corresponding months of two years ago. This was a loss
of $20.933,891 or 91.5 per cent. Between the two periods of time this item's share in
1 See Appendix, (A).
-6-

=== Page 243 === (confidence 0.99)
total value of U. S. war-material sales to Japan fell from 19.77 per cent to 2.04
per cent.
A contimuous downward trend is also noted in sales of automobiles, parts and
accessories. These sales totaled $9,414,586 in the eight months of 1937, but dropped
to $5,463,511 in the corresponding months of 1938 and thence further declined to
$3,733,476 during the same months of 1939. Whereas during the eight months of 1937
this item constituted 8.12 per cent of the total war-material sales, the share dropped
to 3.91 per cent in the comparable 1939 period.
It should be remarked that, even admitting the factor of Japan's foreign
exchange stringency, the heavy curtailment of shipments that occurred in the two items
just dealt with could not have taken place if there had not been a very marked in-
crease in the volume of metal-working machinery supplied by the United States to Japan.
American machine tools have been chiefly instrumental in the development of both the
Japanese automobile industry and the iron and steel works. And it was precisely
through the increased production of domestic plants that Japan was able to drastically
cut down its imports of the items in question.
In the case of aircraft and parts, shipments totaling $2, 279,504 during the
first eight mcnths of this year, although representing a gain of $1,163,517 or 104.26
per cent compared with the corresponding l937 figure, constituted a heavy reduction of
5,183,346 or 69.46 per cent from the 1938 level. This reduction was entirely at-
tributable to the United States "moral embargo", effective since June 1l, 1938, on the
sale to Japan of finished aircraft and aircraft engines. Table 4 shows that during
the eight-month period of this year only one plane, valued at $32,0o0, was shipped to
Japan, as compared with 51 planes, valued at $2,357,418, in the corresponding 1938
period, while exports of airplane engines have entirely disappeared from this year's
figures.
Partioular attention is called to ferro-alloy exports which possess the uni-
que distinotion among the eight major items of having shown the highest rate of 1ncrease.

In point of importance ferro-alloys ranked sixth in the l939 period, as compared with
a poor tenth place in the corresponding period of 1937 (see Table 3).
As indispensible accessory materials in the manufacture of iron and steel,
ferro-alloys are very essential to Japan's war industries. Supplies from this country
largely consist of molybdenum and concentrates, and other ores and concentrates. But,
lack of tungsten, and must rely on imports to fill its own vital requirements. For
this reason the metal is listed by the U. S. Army and Navy Munitions Board as one of
the most important strategic materials. Any further sale during the present war
emergency, however small the amount, will cause grave concern on the part of the

=== Page 245 === (confidence 0.97)
As for the group of ten minor commodities only lead, hides and skins are of
importance, with sales during the eight months of this year amounting, respectively, to
1,892,261 and $1,251,389. It is of interest, however, to note the increasing ship-
ments of scrap rubber from this country to Japan. Exports of this item for the eight-
month interval this year rose in quantity to 38,906,1ll pounds, valued at $547,187,
representing a gain of 430 per cent and 36l per cent over the corresponding totals of
1938 and 1937. The fact that Japan was turning to the use of scrap rubber is a clear
indication of that country's growing foreign exchange stringency. Scrap rubber cur-
rently sells for about 1.5 cents per pound, while one pound of crude rubber costs
about 20.5 cents.
Also of interest is the vanishing magnitude of the item of arms and ammunition
which amounted to some 32 odd dollars for the entire eight months of this year. In
fact, direct munitions, including arms, amminition and aircraft and parts, constituted
only 2.4 per cent of all war materials sold to Japan by the United States in the eight-
month period of 1939, compared with 7.7 per cent and 1.0 per cent for the comparable
periods of 1938 and 1937.
