RECORD OF PROGRESS
Beginnings
The invasion of China started on July 7, 1937. But it was
not until the follcwing spring that trade statistics revealed Japan's
extensive dependence upon the Uinited States for essential war materials.
A growing recognition that America was, in effect, a powerful ally of
Japan for her program of conquest in Asia led to the spontaneous organ-
ization of The American Committee for Non-Participation in Japanese
Aggression. A planned program of work was hegun on August 1, 1938.
Objectives
The following paragraphs are taken from a full statement of
the Committee's objeotives published at that time.
"Neither widespread sympathy for the Chinese people
in their courageous struggle for freedom nor protests over
inhiman warfare and the violation of treaties can atone for
the positive support which we are lending to the Japanese
military machine.
"This Comnittee's reason for existence is to help in
bringing the essential facts to the attention of the American
people, cooperating with other individuals and organizations
that are wcrking to this end, and to investigate ways and
means whereby the stoppage of war credits and essential war
supplies to Japan can be made effective for the remainder of
the present confliot in China.
"The Committee will endeavor to helo in crystallizing
public demand and support for governmental executive action
designed to check the flow of American credits and war mater-
ials to Japan. Study is teing made of the forms of effective
xeoutive action possible under existing law. Contact will
maintained with the governmental departments conoerned.
"The Committee will also work toward seouring the enact-
ment of a measure at the next session of Congress prohibiting
the extension of economio aid for war purposes to a country
whioh is in process of violating a solemn treaty of peace to
which the United States is a carty.
"The central and single aim of the Committee is expressed
in the phrase 'nan-participation in Japanese aggression.'
Doctrinal isms, domestio issues, and the European situation,
however important, are beyond its soope.

=== Page 258 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
"Members of the Committee regard this progran as a
constructive effort in the direution of international peace
and justice. They desire the well-being of the people of
Japan as well as of all other peoples in the Pacific area.
They desire peace and justioe for all concerned. They feel,
at the same time, that before any just settlement and lasting
peace structure can be achieved, Japanese military aggression
must be ohecked. They believe that for this a policy of non-
cooperation by the powers together with continued Chinese
resistance will be effective, and that the urgent and crucial
need of the moment is to withhold economic assistance for war
purposes from Japan."
Study of the Prcblem
With the assistance of outstanding authorities, a careful
study was made of the practical questions involved in the placing of
restraint upon American war trade with Japan. A summary discussion of
prinoipal facts and issues was presented in the Committee's first put-
lication, "America's Share in Japan's War Guilt," which received wide-
spread comment. Continued ccunsel with experts on the Far East and on
American international relations has remained an indispensable part of
the Committee's program.
Enliatment of Workers
It was early realized that for an effective, nation-wide
movement to develop, it would be necessary to invite and enlist the
cooperation of other organizations and of thousands of interested,
informed individuals in all parts of the country. Seventy organiza-
tions and publications were individually approached for carefully
selected names and addresses; the response was heartening. This
furnished a "master file" of 22,000 individuals to Whom were sent
copies of "America's Share in Japan's War Guilt." From answers to
this mailing, a beginning was made in the tuilding of a selected corps
of activo "frionds and supporters." The process begun in this way
has been continuaus ever since. New mailings to new groups have fol-
lowed eaoh other in steady suocession. The Committee now has a master
file of nearly 100,000 key names, over 12,000 of whom have shown an
interest suffioient to enable us to place then in our active file of
friends and supportera. Probably the greatest single asset of the
Committee up to the present time has been this informed and coordinated
body of frionds in every part of the nation, among whom are many people
of outstanding influence, national or in their own communities.

=== Page 259 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
The Committee has felt it exoeedingly important to maintain
a living relationship with this constantly growing list of friends
and supporters. A suecession of thirty mailings, each containing fresh
information, literature, and suggestions for action, have been sent to
the members of this group, many of whom heve beoome, in turn, centers
of information and influence in their own communities. It has been
possible in this way to realize substantial and fooussed expressions
of public opinion at just the times when these would accomplish the
most. The volume of individual personal correspondence that has grown
out of these efforts has been rather overwhelming. Form replies have
been inoreasingly used. But the Committee has felt the importanoe of
keeping relationships personal and not stereotyped, and from 2Co to
300 oarefilly written personal letters are now sent out weekly.
Leadership
As the Committee has established itself and demonstrated
soundness and responsibility in its work, it has been able to secure,
inoreasingly, nationally respeoted leadership. Serving with Mr. Henry
L. Stimson, honorary chairman of the Committee, are the following
honorary vice-chairmen: Ir. Henry I. Harriman, former president of
the U. S. Chamber of Commerce: Dr. A. Lawrence Lowell, president
emeritus of Harvard University; Dr. Robert E. Speer, former president
of the Federal Council of Churches; Mr. Wiiliam Allen White, dean among
American newspaper publishers: and Rear Admiral H. E. Yarnell (retired),
former commander-in-chief of the U. S. Asiatio Fleet. These and many
other nationally known and respeoted citizens have come to the aotive
support of the Committee's program. In important ways, beyond the
lending of their names, they have contributed greatly to the develop-
ment of an informed publio opinion.
Cooperating Local Comnittees
About eighty local committees cooperating aotively with
The Amerioan Committee have developed. Among partioularly strong
committeus are those in Roston, Baltimore, Riohmond, Durham (N. C.),
Pittsburgh, Cleveland, Toledo, Chioago, Kansas City, Minneapolis,
Denver, Seattle, Portland, San Francisoo, and Ios Angeles. The con-
tinuous interohange of information and suggestions between cooperating
committees and the national organization have been exceedingly helpful.
These committees have been especially active in enlisting looal leader-
ship, setting up meetings, distributing literature, seouring newspaper
and radio publioity, and extending greatly the volume of genuine popular
demand for restriotions upon our decisive help to Japan.

=== Page 260 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
Other Cooperating Organizations
The American Committee has endeavored to make itself as useful
as possible to other national and local organizations interested in
Amerioa's international relations and, when practicable, to use existing
channels for the distribution of information and the enlistment of posi-
tive publio support. These organizations have included churoh groups,
business assooiations, civic and prcfessional clubs, labor unions,
women's organizations, foreign policy study groups, and the like. To
date, national and lccal organizations representing a total membership
of well over 40,C00,000 people have fcrmally protested against American
aid to Japan (or, in some cases, to aggressors in general) for war pur-
poses. The effort has been made to cooperate with each organization in
a way consistent with its particular background, basis of interest, and
organizational structure.
Literature
Probably no activity of the Committee has been more basic than
the preparaticn and wide distribution of informative and arresting liter-
ature. There follows a brief summary of publications to date, with approx-
imate figures for the circulation of each - through sales, mail distribution,
special meetings, and organicational channels.

Mailings to Special Groups
Special mailings have helped to oxtend discussion through
the use of material adapted to the interests of particular groups.
The largest such mailing was to the 102,000 Protestant ministers of
America. Others have been sent to: ohief editorial writers of lead-
ing newspapers, prominent cotton growers and brokers, steel producers,
members of the Republican Progran Committee, branches of The American
Union for Concerted Peace Effcrts, editors of Cathclic papers, American
missionaries from China, friends of Chinese education, writers and
speakers on the Far East and on international affairs.
Speakers
Lacking the necessary funds and personnel for setting up a
fill-fledged Speakers' Bureau, the Committee has nevortheless heen
instrumental in arranging several hundred speaking engagements for
competent interpreters of the Far Eastern situation. Inquiries con-
cerning speakers are now handled daily.
Press Pubiicity
The two greatest channeis of public informetion on current
problems are, of course, the press and the radio. Trying to avoid the
elements of emotionel or distorted propaganda, the Comnittee has sent
out 48 factual newspaner releases. Most of these have been utilized
either nationally or locally. We are now finding the press increasingly
receptive to releases from the Committee. A few special releases have
been sent to trade journals, and to church papers.
Letters have also been sent to editors, enclosing materials
suitable for editorial comment, and offering to furnish a few categories
of additional information upon request. This has resulted in inquiries
from a number of newsnapers. Interviews have also been arranged with
editors of leading newspapers in important centers. A luncheon recently
given for publishers and editors of the New York metropolitan area was
attended by aporoximately 30 guests. Several cooperating committees
are now undertaking to arrange similar luncheons.

=== Page 262 === (confidence 0.97)
-F-
Radio Publicity
The Comnittee and cooperating organizations have been directly
instrumental in ar: anging for presentation of the subject of America's
Far Fistern policy over ten national network programs, including the
Town Meeting of the Air, the People's Platferm, the Chicago Round-Table.
Through factual materinl ai personal interviews, basic information has
been furnished to radio commentaters. Between 100 and 15) local broad-
casts have been arranged. Two electricnl transeriptions and several brief
scripts have also been made available to many locel stations.
Interviows
D'ring a recent serveraution, un influertinl newspaper publisher
st essed the inno tance of conti:ne s and effective aooroach to the
"fountain heads" of influence. Jpecial interviews have been conduoted
with leading editors, business men, lawuere, churchnen, and other influ-
ential citirens in i portant centers. It is iot nossihle to estimate the
effeets of these interviews. une illustration, however, might be given.
Interviews with publisters an! eiitorial writers of five leading New York
newspapers have rusuited in the use of materials furnished by the Committee,
and have contributed to an inerensingly streng and clearly focussed editorial
policy in each case.
Financin
From the outset, the Co.mittee has faced the constant necessity
of meeting its operating ex enses through the solicitation of gifts and,
to a m ch less extent, through the sale of literature and a small net
income from spcakers.
A proof of growing popular support of the Committee's position
is found in responses to mail appuals for contributions. Seventeen finan-
cial appeals have been mailed to carefully selected and prepared lists:
the total number of letters sent cut hns been rearly 25o,O00. Mioh care
has gone into the prenaration of these letters in order that in every case
they might prove stimilating and informing, whether or not they yielded a
financial return. These efforts have been supplemented by interviews with
individuals, during which it was possible to explain more fully the work
and needs of the Committee. A number of larger contributions have resulted
from these interviews, and it anpears that an increasing number of substan-
tial gifts will be needed if the Committee's program is to go forward in a
vigorous way during the period which may elapse before adequate measures
are taken by our Governnent to curb America's decisive war aid to Japan.

=== Page 263 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
National Headquarters
With the rapid growth of the Committee's program, it has been
necessary to develop a satisfactory division of labor and systematic
procedures for efficiency and economy. The result has been a volume and
quality of output which, according to three or four critical and experi-
enced office executives, compares favorably with that of the better busi-
ness firms. The interest and morale of the staff has remained oxceedingly
high, even during periods when the pressure of work was overwhelming.
There are now l9 full-time and 5 part-time workurs.
Growth of Public pinion
that effect has this progran of work had  pon public opinion
in the "nited states? Eighteen months ago a member of the State Department
said to a visitor from this Committee that the American public opinion was
apathetic toward the conflict in Asin. Many letters were being received
about Spain, but, although there was general sympathy with China, there
seemed to be no very articulate public opinion nor any effective focussing
of support in favor of a clear line of policy.
Since then there has teen a marked change. It is believed that
the Committee's work and that of cooperating individuals and organizations
heve had a part in this deveiopment. Chjective evidences of developing
attitudes among the American people are to he found in public opinion
polls end in newspaper editorial columns. The Gallup and Fortune polls
have indiceted a steady mounting of disapproval for Japan's course in
China and a mounting sentiment in favor of the cutting off of Anerican
war aid to Japan. A Galluo poll taken during August 1939 showed 82% of
those registering an opinion as favoring a refusal to sell Japan any more
war materials. Despite the European Vur, and the caution revealed by the
Administration, another Gallup poll taken during February 1940 showed 75%
of those voting as definitely favoring a stoppage of war supplies to Japan.
Among the many newspapers which have given positive editorial
support to such a policy during the pest year are:
The Austin Texan
The New York Herald Tribune
The Asheville (N.C.) Citizen
The New York Times
The Bristol (Va.) Herald-Courier
The New York Daily News
The Burlington (Vt.) Free Press

=== Page 264 === (confidence 0.99)
.-8-
A further eviderce of the growth of an active and artioulate
publio opinion has come from Washington itself. embers of the State
Dopartmont and of Congress speak now of strong public support for the
policies advocated by this Committee. Typical of such comment was a
remark of Congressman Bruce Barton during a hearing of the Foreign
Affairs Committee. He said that there surely could be no doubt in the
minds of any member of the Committee, in view of the hundreds of letters
they were receiving daily, that the overwhelming sentiment of the American
people was in favor of a stoppage of American help to Japan's program in
China.
Nashington Relations
It is believed that the Committee's crk -- and the public
demand that it has helped to mobilize -- have contrituted directly and
substantially to the measures already taken ty our Gcvernment:
Adoption and extension of the "moral embargo"
Denunciation of the 19ll commercial treaty
Ambassador Grew's warning to the Japanese people
Development of additional credits to China for
economic rehahilitation.
The Committee will contin'e to sucoort suct. measures until the basic
conditions for a just peace and for a new period of cooperation and
reconstruction in the Pacific area are realized.
Friendly relations have been cons:stently maintained with key
members of the Administration and of Congress. Discussion has taken place
at frequent intervals since the outset of the Conmittee's activities. The
rapid growth in public opinion and public desire for action has led to
increased consultation with government leaders. Several authorities
recommended by the Anerican Committee were invited to testify during hear-
ings conducted by the Foreign Relations and Foreign Affairs committees of
Congress. The effort hes been made to work in harmony with those carrying
the enormous responsitilities involved in the conduot of our foreign re-
lations at this time, while retaining full independence of thought and
action. Peartening assurances have been received with regard to the
reputation which the Committee now has among leaders of both parties and
in the executive branch of the government.
With the establishment of an office in Washington, under the
supervision of Mr. Roger S. Greene, which wili facilitate the conduct of
interviews by able and informed friends and supporters of the Committee,
it is expected that our Washington relations will be further strengthened.
A word about the philosophy underlying our Washington relations
may be in order. Broadly, it might be said that three main influences
determine the development of specific policies in a demooracy. First,
there is the continuing and changing heritage of information and mis-
information, sentiment and prejudice which exists throughout the nation
on any question of publio concern. Second, there is the constant impact

=== Page 265 === (confidence 0.99)
-9-
of the news, with its effect upon pop:lar attitides end upon the intensity
of public interest. And third, there is conscious effort on the part of
interested groups to influence both public opinion and the course of
government policy. In a dynamic democracy there is continuous interplay
among these complicated influences.
The last named influence is frequently called "pressure". The
arts of propagenda and of political influence have become sufficiently
advanced to enable pressure groups to operate with powerful effect in a
democracy. These groups are, broadly speaking, of two types: those which
repres-nt private or specinl group interests, seeking to maintain or ad-
vance their own adventeges in any situation: and those which represent a
broader public interest, seeking to realize what might be termed the
greatest good to the greatest number. The fi'st type is more readily
organized. The second, becaune it cuts ncross all grouns, is less readily
mobilized and made articulate ond effective. The survival and healthy
functioning of a democracy may depend pon the sbility of those who take
the larger view to think end act realistically, with sufficient clarity,
unity. and strength to make their views effective. The competitive inter-
play of special interests is not onough. f course, there are honest
differences of opinior. anong those whe take a larger view, and these must
be tested on the anvils of oublic disc ssion and debate.
Those working for the objectives of The American Committee for
Non-Participation in Japanese Ag ression have. for the most part, endeavored
to see the Far rastern situntion and American Far Lastern policies as a
whole, and to work for a solution which, in the long run, would be of great-
est benefit to all concerned, including the Japanese people themselves, and
which would be consistent witl. the requirements of a more just and durable
pee ce.
From this standpoint, the Committee's work has been focussed,
first, toward the spread of substantial information and interest, and
second, toward a realistic and effective mohilization of public opinion
in support of political action. ho stereotyped formulae have been foisted
upon the public. kether, an attempt has been made to provide basic in-
formation, with interpretation from a viewpoint clearly stated, and to
invite honest, individuel expressions of opinion whether or not they
agreed with the position taken by the Committee. The result has been,
we believe, a more thoughtful end convincing presentation of views to
members of Congress and the Administration than would heve been achieved
through inflated, high-pressure methods. Mention has already been made
of the extent of public supnort for the curtailment of war aid to Japan,
and it is believed that this nation-wide demand has been wholly genuine.

PRCGRAA AND NEEDS
Introductory Note
The attempt, in a short period of time, to place a great
issue in international relations effectively before the American
people is no small undertaking. During the past eighteen months a
good deal has been learned, through trial and error, about the most
effective methods of work and the relative importance of various
aporoaches. The program covered in the following pages provides
for a contin ation and extension of work already in hand and for a
number of special projects which, it is believed, will definitely
enhance the effectiveness of the Comnittee's program as a whole.
It is difficult to estimate with assurance the length of
time that will be required before our government places more sub-
stantial and effective restrictions upon our crucial war trade with
Jaoan, or the moments when more intensive effort will be required.
Steps taken up to the present time - the renunciation of the trade
treaty with Japan, Ambassador Grew's warning to the Japanese people,
the extension of the moral embargo, the development of a new credit
for China - have all grown out of public demand for effective nction.
The promptness and effectiveness of further measures which the govern-
ment may feel enabled to take will depend in large measure upon the
steady continuance and mounting of public demand as well as upon
developments in the Far East and in Japanese-American relations.
It is very important that further action be taken promptly.
The aoproach of party conventions and elections, the possibilities
of intensified conflict in Europe, economic and political strains in
China as well as in Japan all point to the need of considered and
decisive measures without undue delay.
Further plans for the Committee's work are based upon an
estimated twenty-week program of vigorous effort, with the belief
that if this program can be carried through successfully, the desired
results will be essentially achieved. The estimated finanoial re-
quirements of such a program are outlined in the pages that follow.

=== Page 284 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
February 13, 1940
MEMORANDUM TO SECRETARY HOPKINS
The situation with regard to Japan's dependence upon this
country for aviation gasoline seems to be as follows:
1- An aeroplane will operate quite satisfactorily on gasoline
much under i00-octane, the standard adopted by the United
States Army. It is probable that most of the aviation
gasoline now being sent abroad is not over 87-octane,
similar to that being used by the British Army. At the
present time there are practically no plants of the
kind needed to prcduce a very high cctane gas, outside
the United States.
2- The best available information indicates that Japan
is for all practical purposes entirely dependent upon
the United States for 87-l00-octane gasoline; that
this grade of gasoline can not be bought in quantity
elsewhere; and that neither the requisite grade of
crude petroleum nor the necessary equipment to make
such gasoline is available in Japan or to Japan else-
where than in the United States.
3 Statistics relating to Japan's petroleum industry
and trade have never been satisfactory; and since 1937,
publication of these figures has ceased, such information
being considered a military secret.
The accompanying table indicetes what crude petroleum
and gasoline is thought could have been made available in
Japan during 1937, 38, and 39. It is the consensus of
opinion of oil and military men that Japanese imports of
these products have substantially exceeded consumption;
and that there is little, if any, unused storage capacity
now in Japan.
4 Japan has built up a fair-sized petroleum refining
industry. Daily "throughput" for straight-run refining
1s said to be 52,600 barrels; while that for cracking
1s estimated at 16,250 barrels. At least two of Japan's
recent cracking installations (capacity not known) should
be capable of producing gasoline of 78-octane. This
with an addition of lead could be brought up to 82-octane,
which 1s probably satisfactory for Japanese planes.
5- Japan 1s largely dependent upon imports of crude
petroleum. Approximately 90 percent of the petroleum
refined in Japan is of foreign origin; and of this,
estimates indicate that 80 to 85 percent comes from
the United States. Principal other suppliers are
Sakahlin, Netherlands Ind:es, and British Borneo; al-
though crude oil has also been sent to Japan from Mexico,
Eouador, Peru, and Bahrien Island (in the Persian Gulf).
6

=== Page 285 === (confidence 1.00)
-2
6- Gasoline under 87-octane and the crude petroleum
from which to make it can be purchased therefore by
Japan from sources other than the United States.
Moreover, Japan possesses a very respectable fleet
of tankers with which to transport these oils. Stop-
page of American exports of petroleum and gasoline
(omitting consideration of aviation gasoline) would,
however, work a hardship on Japan. Bringing oils from
further distance than the United States Texcept
Netherlands Indies) would enhance the cost of trans-
port. Also, the Japanese refining industry is built
to operate largely on California crude oil, and use
of crudes from other sources would probably mean
decreased efficiency.
7- Indications are that under present fighting condi-
tions in China, Japanese planes can perform quite
satisfactorily on a good grade of automobile gasoline.
If Japan should become involved in war with a first-
class military power, it would be handicapped in the
air without high-grade gasoline; and it is probably
for this reason she has made efforts to build up
reserve stocks of this kind. It is believed that
with Japan's present stocks and present rate and type
of consumption, if her supplies from this country
were cut off she would be able to continue the war
in China at least another year without serious danger
of a shortage of petroleum products.
James W. Young
Director

ADVISER ON POLITICAL RELATIONS
March 5, 1940.
Mr. Secrétary:
It was I who called especially to
your attention figures by the Department
of Commerce indicating that U.S. exports
of high-test gasoline to Japan during
January 1940 had been almost as great as
during the year 1939.
It now proves that these figures
were completely in error. On the
basis of figures now presented by the
Department of Commerce, the exports
during January 1940 would be about
one twenty-fourth or the amount of
those of 1939.
Hereafter, in citing Department
of Commerce figures, I shall be
inclined to say, "if correct".

Mr. Biscaye of C. Tennant Sons & Co., Nine Rockefeller
8
94.24/856
Plaza, telephoned from New York yesterday and made inquiry
concerning the exportation to Japan of twenty-five tons
of Chinese antimony. Mr. Biscaye said that his company
imported the antimony in bond for speculation and that
9
under the present proposal it would be sold to Mitsui for
export in order to obviate the necessity of paying an im-
port duty thereon. He added that his company has in the
past made similar exports of antimony to Japan and that
the transaction might very well therefore be classed
as a normal peacetime export. Upon inquiry, however, it
developed that prior exports of this commodity to Japan
were all made since the commencement of hostilities between
ED
to Japan prior to that time, most of the exports having
0
takon place in 1937 and 1938.
F/N
I

=== Page 295 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
I informed Mr. Biscaye that the exportation of antimony
to Japan or to any other foreign country at the present time
would not, generally speaking, be consistent with the polioy
announced by the Army and Navy Munitions Board on October 1l,
1939. I added, however, that the facts that the shipment
had been imported in bond and that his company had made
exports of this commodity to Japan prior to the outbreak
of the present conflict in Europe raised certain questions
which I should like to disouss with other officers of the
Department before giving him a further reply in regard
thereto.
After consultation with Mr. Veatch of EA, I telephoned
Mr. Biscaye and informed him that it was the Department's
feeling that the mere fact that the shipment was imported
in bond would not necessarily take the shipment out of
the category of objectionable exports. I mentioned the
fact also that the exportation in question could scarcely
be viewed as a normal peacetime export, since it appeared
that the company's prior exports of antimony to Japan were
due to unusual oiroumstances. Mr. Biscaye agreed that this
type of export could not be classed as normal. I suggested
to mr. Biscaye that the Department would be pleased to give
further

=== Page 296 === (confidence 0.93)
-3-
further consideration to this matter if he would submit
in writing complete information concerning his company's
prior exports of antimony to Japan, including quantities
and dates. Mr. Biscaye left with me the impression that
the antimony in question would not be sold for export to
Japan. He said that he would be glad to submit, for the
Department's information, the facts concerning his company's
prior exports.

Malch 22 1940
My dearr. Secretary:-
Denartment of S'ate
Recently I have received a mumber of letters from both
individuals and organisations protesting against the sale of war
894.24
materials to Japan and in some cases urging that Congress place an
embargo upon the sale of war materials to Japan. In my answers, I
have consistently taken the position that the extent and the variety
894.24/862
of the interests involved render it expedient to leave these
questions to the judgment of the State Department with its greater
and more detailed knowledge. I have pointed out that legislation
limiting the freedom of action of the State Department might easily
prevent results beneficial to China and to the cause of peace. In
other words, I have in the past defended you and the State Depart-
ment and I am confident that there must be sound and humane bases
for the course of action during the past months. What, these bases
are, however, is unknowm to me.
These letters, Mr. Secretary, are from good Christian
people who are shocked and horrified to find our government following
a policy by which our government actually furnishes very tangible
aid to a nation which by all available evidence is the harsh aggressor
in an armed conflict against a weaker nation. Their views are un-
doubtedly shared by thousands of others in my District who have not
actually communicated with me. My own judgment, and sentiment are
wholeheartedly in accord with their judgment and sentiment.
In behalf of these Constituents of mine and on behal of
myself both as the Representative in Congress from the 19th Congreasional
District of Pennsylvania and as an American Citizen, I ask you to do
all that you can to end quickly this unchristian policy now being
pursued. I ask you further to state, insofar as it is possible for you

The term "high grade" aviation gasoline may have a different
meaning to different people, depending on its particular use and variation
in ongine design in different countries. In general, the octane number of
"high grade" aviation gasoline in different countries may vary as follows:
United States - l00 O.N.
England
- 100
France
- 95
Japan
-90←92
Germany
87
Russia
These octane numbers assume the inolusion of tetra ethyl lead up to 6 co's
par U.s. gallon.
Beside the ootane number "high grade" aviation gasoline
specifications may vary in other respects, boiling range and heat test.
These specifications would exolude thermally cracked (2) and reformed (3)
gasolines but with further treatment these may be used as aviation fuels in
some countries and olassed as lower grade than that considered under "high
grade" aviation gasolines, according to U.S.A., British and French standards.
Diesel fuels may also be classed as aviation fuels where
Diesel aviation motors are used (Germany ?). Germany is also reported to
have designed and in use a direct fuel injection system similar to fuel
injeetion into Diesel engines, which enables them to use a lower octane
aviation fuel than that required for engines using the conventional
carburetor system.
The raw material used in the so-oalled iao-octane plants,
under construction by us in the U.S.S.R. and Japan, is derived from the
oporation of cracking plants (2) and (3), either thermal or catalytie.
The produot of these plants is the base material and main component of
"high grade" aviation gasoline. Your recent letter we interpreted as
giving us pemission for additional oonstruotion of these oracking units.
We desire you to have all this information so that you may
evaluate your present polioy, now limited to the iso-ootane steps (6) in
the produotion of aviation fuel.

Mr. H. J. Halle, President of the Universal Oil
Products Company, accompanied by Dr. Gustav Egloff, tech-
nical expert of the company, and Mr. L. N. Healy, Mr.
Halle's secretary, called at my office this morning.
Mr. Henderson of Eu participated in the conversation.
Mr. Halle referred to his letter of January 30 and
asked me whether the technical information contained
therein had been of interest to the Department.
PS/MFM
I replied in the affirmative, saying that those of
30
us who were called upon to deal with this question of
moral embargo on the delivery of plans, plants, manu-
facturing rights, or technical information required fog
the produotion of high quality aviation gasoline to certain
countries

=== Page 321 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
countries were endeavoring to assimilate as much technical
information about the matter as we could. I said that I
must point out, however, that none of us was capable of
mastering the various technical problems involved and that
therefore, when any of the interested companies had re-
quested a ruling as to whether any proposed transaction
would or would not contravene the policy agreed upon at
the meeting in my office on December 14, 1939, and set
forth in the Department's press release of December 20,
we had been obliged to reply that the companies themselves,
upon which we rely to carry out the Government's policy,
must decide such questions in the light of their own
superior technical knowledge.
Mr. Halle spoke emphatically of his desire to co-
operate loo% with this Government in the carrying out of
any policy which it should decide upon. Nevertheless,
he felt that our practice of leaving decisions as to
whether any particular proposed transaction would contra-
vene the policy announced on December 20 was certain to
lead to unintentional unfairness and to difficulties
among the interested companies. He pointed out that
the plant which his company was considering setting up
in Japan would be a oracking plant essentially similar
to the plant which the Lummus Company had been engaged
1n

=== Page 322 === (confidence 0.99)
-3
in setting up in the Soviet Union and from which the
latter company had withdrawn its engineers on the as-
sumption that to leave them in the Soviet Union would
contravene the Government's policy. (It may be noted
parenthetically that the Lummus Company withdrew its
engineers on its own initiative although its officers
were well aware of the fact that the construction of
the plant in question would not contravene the policy
of this Government as set forth in the press release
of December 20.) He said that he did not believe that
the setting up of the proposed plant in Japan by his
company would contravene the Government's policy but,
nevertheless, he did not wish to enter into any trans-
action which might reeult in his company's being accused
of unfairness to its competitors.
Mr. Halle and Dr. Egloff then entered into a tech-
nical explanation of the means whereby the Soviet Union
and the Japanese Government are now obtaining high quality
aviation gasoline. They said that the Soviet Government
could, by various methods already knowm to Soviet eng1-
neers, produce from oils available in the Union a sufficient
quantity of reasonably good aviation gasoline. The Japan-
ese, on the other hand, are dependent upon imports of crude
oil, ordinary gasoline, and blending agents from the United
States.

=== Page 323 === (confidence 0.97)
-4一
States. With these commodities in hand, they can, by
methods already known to them, produce aviation gasoline
thlt
of sufficiently high quality for his needs. In fact,
they are now producing in this manner the aviation
gasoline which they are using. If the Universal Oil
Products Company or other companies set up cracking
plants in Japan, these plants would produce nothing
more than high grade motor gasoline but, by blending,
the Japanese could transform this gasoline into high
quality aviation gasoline. Thus, the setting up of
cracking plants is one step in the production of high
quality aviation gasoline. Mr. Halie summed up by saying
that the moral embargo policy of this Government was
utterly 1neffective to produce the results which he
presumed were the desired results; that we had locked
the front door and left the back door wide open; and
that, if we wished to prevent the Japanese from obtain-
ing the high quality aviation gasoline which they were
now using to bombard the Chinese civilian populatione,
we should place an embargo on the exportation from this
country to Japan of crude oil, ordinary gasoline, and
blending agents, and prevent all American companies from
proceeding to carry out their present contracts and from
entering

=== Page 324 === (confidence 0.98)
-5..
entering into any further contracts to set up plants in
Japan for the production of high grade motor gasoline.
Mr. Halle mentioned in passing that his company, on
its own initiative, had made the moral embargo already in
effect applicable to Germany, and he said that he could
not understand why the policy was not definitely made ap-
plicable to Germany as well as to Japan and the U.S.S.R.
I stated that, from our long discussion, there emerged
two practical questions:
(1) Should this Government 1mmediately proclaim a
moral embargo on the exportation of oil products to Japan?
and
(2) Would any useful purpose be served by calling
another meeting of the interested companies with a view
to endeavoring to clear up any possible misunderstanding
as to the scope of the moral embargo already in effect?
I sa1d that, as far as the first question was concerned,
we were, as he knew, making a careful study of exports
of oil products to Japan and the U.S.S.R. I did not
believe, however, that there was any possibility that
the appropriate authorities of this Government would con-
sider it advisable at this time to extend the embargo in
respect to Japan so as to include discouragement of the
exportation

=== Page 325 === (confidence 0.97)
-6-
exportation of oil products. In regard to the second
question, Mr. Henderson and I said that we would place
his suggestion before our superiors and that I would
inform him in the near future whether or not they con-
sidered it advisable to call another meeting of repre-
sentatives of the interested companies.
Mr. Halle said that he believed that such a meeting
would serve a useful purpose. He added that the companies
were bitter competitors and that they would never be able
to arrive at any understanding among themselves except at
a meeting presided over by an officer of the Department at
which the wishes of the Department were made clear. He
suggested that, if e meeting were called, representatives
should be summoned from all of the companies represented
at the meeting on December 14 and, in addition, representa-
tives from the Texas Oil Company and the Lummus Company.
Iaqvee
In my opinion, it is extremely doubtful whether the
BuH(Eu)
calling at this time of such a meeting as Mr. Halle sug-
gested is either necessary or advisable. The real question
for discussion at such a meeting would be whether or not
the

=== Page 326 === (confidence 0.95)
一7一
the moral embargo announc ed on December 20 should be ex-
tended to include the delivery of plans, plants, manufac-
turing rights, or technical information required for the
production of high grade motor gasoline for the reason
that such gasoline can be transformed by blending into
high quality aviation gasoline. No agreement could be
reached among the companies which would not involve furthe r
breaches of contracts already entered into. Should the
time ever come when this Government really wishes to make
1t extremely difficult for the Japanese to obtain suitable
aviation gasoline, that result can be obtained by an
embargo on the exportation of oil products.
Joseph C. Green

ON POLITICAL

Dr. Stanley K. Hornbeck

Department of State
DEP RTMENT OF STATE
Washington, D. C.
Dear Dr. Hornbeck:
As a citizen of the United States, resident in Cnina since 1926,
I am calling upon you to aak that you do all in your power to prevent the
sale of war materials and equipment by American citizens, to Japan.
The best interests of the United States, as well as humanity and
justice, call for an embargo on war materials and equipment now going from
this country to Japan. The same reasons demand that no American credits
be available to Japan.
Six reasons for these strong convictions of mine are given below.
These convictions are based upon personal observations in cities and rural
areas during my residence in China, which has included two years of the
present war.
894.24/869
Very truly yours,
894.24
Thendore C. Greena
Theodore C. Greene, M.D.
School of Medicine, Cheeloo University
Tsinan, Shantung, China
1. If Japan succeeds in holding and consolidating her coastal
conquests in China, she will try to go on from one conquest to
another in the Pacific Ocean and adjacent areas. She will become
MAR 1 4 1940
a menace to the peace of the Pacific Ocean and of the United
FILED
States. In this case, we shall require a navy considerably larger
than now planned.
2. Japan will never conquer the interior provinces of China,
nor will she control the areas between the railroads and larger
cities of the coastal regions. But with American war supplies
available, she can hold certain points along the China coast for a
considerable period of time, -- long enough to establish naval
F/A
bases and to menace the peace of the Pacific Ocean. With Japan
receiving American war supplies, it might take China several tens

=== Page 333 === (confidence 0.98)
-2
of years to become strong enough in the interior to drive
Japan out of the coastal provinces. War will continue in
China until the armed forces of Japan are driven out.
Without American war supplies, Japan cannot retain the
coastal provinces of China for more than a few years, -- not
long enough to develop effective naval bases.
3. The best course to follow in order to avoid war between
the United States and Japan is the passage of an embargo to
prevent the sale by our citizens, of war materials and equipment
to Japan. The best guarantee of the peace of the Pacific Ocean
area is a free and independent China.
4. For many reasons, Japanese control of the coast of China
is hurting and will continue to hurt American trade, causing losses
to us far greater in the long run than the losses from an embargo
on war supplies now going to Japan.
5. In signin the Nine Power treaty, we agreed "To provide
the fullest ani most unembarrassed opportunity to China to develop
and maintain for herself an effective ani stable government."
(Second item, first article of the treaty.) Are we living up to
this agreement when we allow our citizens to provide Japan with
the materials and equipment for attempting to overthrow the
government of China, and for attempting to conquer China?
6. It is impossible to comprehend the immensity and intensity
of the sufferings of the Chinese, especially of the civilians, in
this invasion of their country by the armed forces of Japan.
I have seen the refugees, some of the forty or more
millions of them forced from their homes. I, a doctor, have treated
the wounded. And I have seen the extensive use made by the Japanese
forces, of American materials and equipment in this invasion.
Any of the above statements, I shall be glad to discuss further
if wi shed.
Theodne C. Greene.

Mr. Maxwell M. Hamilton
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
Division of Far Eastern Affairs
MAR 5-1940
Department of State
Washington, D. C.
Dear Sir:
As a citizen of the United States, resident in China since 1926,
I am calling upon you to ask that you io all in your power to prevent the
sale of war materials and equipment by American citizens, to Japan.
The best interests of the United States, as well as humanity and
justice, call for an embargo on war materials and equipment now going from
this country to Japan. The same reasone demand that no American credits
be available to Japan.
Six reasons for these strong convictions of mine are given below.
These convictions are based upon personal observations in cities and rural
areas during my residence in China, which has included two years of the
present war.
Very truly yours,
Theodone C. Greene
Theodore C. Greene, M.D.
School of Medicine, Cheeloo University
Tsinan, Shantung, China
MAR 1 4 1940
1. If Japan succeeds in holding and consolidating her coastal
FILED
conquests in China, she will try to go on from one conquest to
another in the Pacific Ocean and adjacent areas. She will become
a menace to the peace of the Pacific Ocean and of the United
States. In this case, we shall require a navy considerably larger
than now planned.
2. Japan will never conquer the interior provinces of China,
nor wilr she control the areas between the railroads and larger
cities of the coastal regions. But with American war supplies
available, she can hold certain points along the China coast for a
considerable period of time, -- long enough to establish naval
bases and to menace the peace of the Pacific Ocean. With Japan
receiving American war supplies, it might take China several tens

=== Page 335 === (confidence 0.98)
-2
of years to become strong enough in the interior to drive
Japan out of the coastal provinces. War will contimue in
China until the armed forces of Japan are driven out.
Without American war supplies, Japan cannot retain the
coastal provinces of China for more than a few years, -- not
long enough to develop effective naval bases.
3. The best course to follow in order to avoid war between
the United States and Japan is the passage of an embargo to
prevent the sale by our citizens, of war materials and equipment
to Japan. The best guarantee of the peace of the Pacific Ocean
area is a free and independent China.
4. For many reasons, Japanese control of the coast of China
is hurting and will continue to hurt American trade, causing losses
to us far greater in the long run than the losses from an embargo
on war supplies now going to Japan.
5. In signing the Nine Power treaty, we agreed "To provide
the fullest ani most unembarrassed opoortunity to China to develop
and maintain for herself an effective and stabls government."
(Second item, first article of the treaty.) Are we living up to
this agreement when we allow our citizens to provide Japan with
the materials and equipment for attempting to overthrow the

6. It is impossible to comprehend the immensity and intensity

=== Page 349 === (confidence 0.99)
TO THE HONORABLE H. P. FULMER
Member of Congress.
Your Petitioners, citizens of Columbia, South Carolina,
respectfully petition and esk that immediate steps be
taken by the United States Government to stop the shipment
of war materials from this country to Japan so long as
Japan continues its aggressions against China.
The grounds justifying this oetition are as follows:
(1) That Japan is waging an undeclared war against
China and the Chinese people in direct violation
of its treaty agreements with the United States and,
therefore, does not deserve any consideration from
this country in the prosecution of this war.
(2) Thet we are reliably informed that a very large
percent, estimated at over eighty percent, of certain
necessary and essential war materials are being sup-
plied from the United States. That these could not be
secured from any other source, under present conditions,
nd, therefore, this country is furnishing Janan with
the means to carry on and prosecute this war.
(3) That Janan is waging an undeclared and unjustified
war of aggression, in violation of the rights of
China as a nation, and also in violation of the treaty
rights of the United States and other Neutrals.
(4) That Javan 1s ruthlessly and wantonly killing
non-combatants, women and children, destroying
property of orivate individuals, bombing towns and
cities, contrary to rules that should govern in civilized
warfare, destroying properties built through long years
of sacrifice by religious and philanthropic organiza-
tions from the United States and other civilized
countries for the relief and benefit of the people of
China, and committing acts of warfare that the United
States should not be a party to, as it is now being made,
by supplying war materials to Japan without which this
war could not last long.
We,therefore, respectfully beg that immediate steps be
taken to discontinue all shipments of essential war
material, scrap iron, erms or equipment from the United
States

=== Page 350 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
States to Japan.
Respectfully submitted,
ROSE W. DYE
(Mrs)
Rose Wall Dye,
Secretary Pro-tem,
Crape Myrtle Garden Club,
Columbia, South Car lina.

=== Page 359 === (confidence 0.98)
ROGER S. GREENE
Room 710
548 LINCOLN STREET
WORCESTER, MASS.
1420 New York Ave
Washington D C
March 27 1940
Dear Mr President:
Does not the approaching inauguration of the
puppet government of Wang Ching-wei in Nanking, furnish a very
appropriate time for further pressure upon Japan?
The granting of an additional credit to China
has helped to strengthen the Chinese morale, and I believe was a
wise act. However, unless something is done soon to reduce the
economic advantage that Japan enjoys from trade with us, the
credit alone may be ineffective and the Japanese army may
consider that the abrogation of the commercial treaty was an
empty gesture which we are afraid to follow up. This may be
dangerous.
I have gained the impression that our govern-
ment might exert pressure through imposition of additional duties
on imports from Japan, thus reducing the amount of foreign ex-
change available for buying war materials abroad, and that a
direct embargo on exports to Japan might be held in reserve to be
applied later if required.
This program ought to produce good results if
it is actually applied, but many people are wondering why the
first steps have not yet been taken in spite of the popular
support that such measures would have.
As for the embargo on exports of war materials,
even if it is not to be applied immediately, should not the power
to impose it be placed in your hands now, before Congress adjourns?
If this is not done now, no embargo would seem to be possible for
another twelve months.
Many people believe that a measure like the
Pittman bill, giving you discretion to impose an embargo, would
pass 1f you and the Secretary of State would signify your approval
to the leaders in the two houses. Some Republicans say that they
would then support it. Could you not consider before you leave
Washington the advisability of giving Senator Pittman the signal
to proceed with his bill? The Chinese are having a hard time carry-
ing on in the face of the economic assistance that the Japanese get
from us, and badly need relief.
Sincerely yours
RogerN.buere
The President
The White House
Washington D C

FAR EISTERN AFFAIRS
March 21, 19
NOTED
APR 2-1940
E：
1940
NOTED
Mr. Irving H. Taylor, Manager of the Export Department
894.24/879
of the Aeronautical Chamber of Commerce of America, Inc.,
called at my office this morning by appointment. He said
that the Chamber had received a "mandate from all its mem-
bers" to take appropriate action to have the embargo on
894.24
airplanes, airplane parts, etc., to Japan removed or modi-
fied. He spoke at considerable length and with great
emphasis in support of his contentions that the embargo
was unfair to the airplane industry in that it disoriminated
against that particular industry; that the embargo was
FILED
R
populations by the Japanese; and that it would result in
depriving this country of an important market for airplanes
and
F/N

=== Page 363 === (confidence 0.98)
—2
and aviation equipment which would probably be taken over
by the Italians. He read me portions of a stenographic
report of a meeting of representatives of the aircraft
industry recently held in New York at which the moral
embargo was discussed. The excerpts which he read to me
were filled with exaggerations, inaccuracies, and downright
misstatements of fact. They did, however, seem to refleot
irritation against the moral embargo and the Department on
the part of some representatives of the aircraft industry.
I told Mr. Taylor that I thought there was little
likelihood that the Government would decide to abolish
the moral embargo in question and that I thought that the
airplane industry was, from the point of view of its own
interests, making a mistake in urging such action. I sug-
gested that the public reaction to an attempt on the part
of the industry to have the embargo removed would probably
prove a boomerang.
Mr. Taylor said that, if the Government insisted on
maintaining the embargo, it should either be modified to
exclude "commercial" planes or should be extended to inolude
all articles and commodities which were useful in warfare.
He said that he proposed to carry on this fight by all
possible means; that, if the Executive did not reverse
1ts present policy in the matter in the very near future,
he

=== Page 364 === (confidence 0.96)
-3
he intended to take the question up with members of
Congress; and that, if that failed, the Aeronautical
Chamber of Commerce and the industry would resort to
publicity.
Mr. Taylor's tone throughout was extremely combative
and highly critical of the Department.
I told Mr. Taylor that I would report what he had
said to my colleagues and superiors but that I did not
believe that there was any likelihood that his protests
would result in the reversal of the policy of this Gov-
ernment in respect to this matter.
Joseph C. Green

Gentlemen:
I hesitate to bother you because of the stress and strain
constantly upon you for answering all sorts of questions from all
sorts of propagandists on every subject. I do not happen to be
interested in any special agency that is promoting anything in the
894.24/88
Far East. I am a University teacher. I recently spent two years as
exchange professor at Nanking. I hope you will be good enough to
send me some information.
What is delaying the moral embargo being put on the export of
aviation gasoline to Japan? We had all supposed that this would
immediately be put into effect following the termination of the
treaty with Japan, but it is this aviation gasoline that the Japanese
army is using so successfully in bombing Chinese villages and which
0
the American people are almost unanimously in favor of stopping. My
question is, what has hindered this moral embargo, and why is it
that the administration is doing nothing about these matters?
In the second place we had all supposed that the administration
would put some type of duty upon the imports of Japanese products
being sold in this country for the purchase of war materials. Surely
it is known by the administration that the sentiment in America is
almost unanimously for some action being taken toward stopping the
sale of war materials to Japan.
Is there anything that ordinary citizens and educators and
others who are concerned with such questions can do in this matter?
Thanking you for your help, I am
1940

=== Page 379 === (confidence 0.97)
REI
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
DEPARIMENT
April 3,
1940
DIVISION OI
RA/H:
With reference to Co's memorandum of
March 30, attached hereto, Mr. Yost, upon in-
quiry, informed me to the effect that the moral
embargo is applicable to all of the materials
comprising the January shipments of aeronautical
supplies to Japan; that none of the materials
included therein would seem to be subject to
export license except the item of wheels for
which a license was granted last November; and
that the Douglas company, which made the ship-
ment of wheels, was, at the time indicated,
questioned by the Department since which time
it has made no further shipments to Japan.
Mr. Yost also said that the fact that during
January shipments were made to Japan of
aeronautical supplies valued at $200,000 does
not necessarily indicate a breakdown in the
effectiveness of our moral embargo in view of
the fact that such shipments could represent
an accumulation of small orders placed by
Japanese firms in the United States with small
supply houses which are not registered with the
Department.
Rem

Dear Sirs:
I notice that the Sumitomo Chemical Company
is asking for bids in America on equipment for the
hydrogenation of coal. In my opinion this proposition
is just as fully under the import of the "moral
embargo" as would be the sale of plants, designs and
technical data for the production of aviation gasoline.
Coal hydrogenation plants, while ordinarily producing
heavy oils, nevertheless indirectly further the
aviation program with all of its possibilities for
uncivilized activities. Japan is yet in a position
to be plenty mischievious, and not only China but the
Philippines are yet to feel their atrocious activities
with steel, high explosives and dope - brave Japan's
newest wartime weapon, directed largely to defenseless
civilian populations.
894.24
Very truly yours,
Chas. G. Ba

Charles O. Brown

Sir:
I have the honor to refer to my despatches Nos.135
and 139, of March 2 and 5, 1940, respectively, in regard
to recent Japanese diplomatic and commercial activities
in Bolivia and to report that these continue to be pressed.
According to local mining interests, approximately
140 tons of wolfram and 500 tons of tin concentrates were
894.24
shipped

=== Page 394 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
shipped from Bolivia to Japan during the month of February.
The principal shippers to Japan appear to have been
Mauricio Hochschild S.A.M.I. and the Banco Minero. The
acting manager of the first states that, from its corres-
pondents in Japan, his company has ascertained that the
tin concentrates shipped to Japan are not being smelted
but are being held in warehouses. He states further that
he has definite information that earlier wolfram shipments,
as well as molybdenum shipments from the United States,
6.21
have been sent on to Germany by the Japanese purchasers
via the Trans-Siberian Railway.
The mining executive in
question asserts that there is no question as to the facts
and that shipment of woliran and molybdenum by the source
indicated would be economically justifiable in view of
Germany's need for these minerals and their relatively high
unit value. The source or this information is incidentally
a German citizen of ryan blood although not friendly to
the present régime in Germany.
The Chilean Ambassador also states in confidence that
he is advised that large amounts of Chilean copper ore
have recently gone to Japan for smelting in Manchukuo and
that parts of these shipments are destined to Germany by
the route mentioned above. The Legation is not inclined
to place as much credence on the German part of the latter
report as on the former although it has no means of check-
ing either locally and reports them for what they are worth.
It would be of interest if the comments of the Embassy in
Tokyo on the situation could be secured. It would be of
14.6352
partioular assistance if it could be ascertained whether
there are any tin smelters in Japan or Manchukuo. Reports
on this point in Bolivia are conflioting.
At

=== Page 395 === (confidence 0.96)
-3-
AIL
At least two Japanese officials not stationed in
Bolivia have recently visited La Paz. A member of the
Japanese Commercial Mission which has recently been in
Chile was here during March for some time, apparently in
connection with ore shipments, at least his contacts
seem mostly to have been with the Banco Minero. The
Japanese Ambassador to Argentina, Mr. Iwataro Uchiyama
(his nane is not accurately known to the Legation but
it was so carried on the passenger manifest), arrived in
La Paz by air from Argentina on March 22 and departed
on his return trip on March 24. Nhat the purpose of his
visit was is not known.
The Japanese Chargé d'Affaires ad interim in Bolivia,
Mr. Minoro Izawa, departed from La Paz about three weeks
ago for his previous post, Lima, Peru, where he had left
his family. He stated that he would return shortly but
there seems to be some doubt on the point among his
acquaintances. Before Mr. Izawa's departure, a Japanese
Consul, a Mr. Sato, understood to be a career officer, was
granted an exequatur and is functioning as such. He is
at least temporarily in charge of Japanese interests in
Bolivia.
Respectfully yours,

THE METHODIST CHURCH
BISHOP JAMES CANNON. JR
April 26 1940
24 NORTH AL EN AVENUE
RICHMOND. VA.
The President,
Ack.2/24/40
The White House,
Washington, D. C.
594.24
Dear Mr. Presidents
w
I wrote to Secretary Hull on September I l9s7, lass than sixty days after Japan,
without justification, with shell and bomb, was carrying destruotion and death to peace-
loving China, extending her mmssacres even to defenseless civilians. In my letter I em-
phasised the appeal of Generalissimo Chiang Kai-Shek for the intervention of foreign
powers to compel Japan to end her unprovoked warfare. Japan is not only a signer of the
Kellogg Peace Pact, but also of the Nine-Power Treaty, which binds the signers to protect
the sovereignty and territoriality of China.
Japan's ruthless conquest of Manchuria branded her as an outlaw among the
nations. This new attempt at the spoliation and domination of a peaceful country, whose
sovereignty she is bound by treaty to protect, is an attack not only upon China, but
upon the interests of all the great nations which trade with China, and which have
commercial, educational, medical and religious investments in that country. The declared
policy of the United States has been, and is now, the "Open Door" in China. But Japan, with
callous contempt not only for China, but for the rights and property of other nations, is
compelling peaceful foreigners to leave China to save their lives; to leave their proper-
8
ties to be destroyed or stolen by Japan, and to termirate their business, educational med-
6
ical and religious activities.
94.24/894
I insisted in that letter of September 1, 1937, that the United States, as a
signer of the Nine-Power Treaty, must declare to Japan that her warfare against China
must cease, and that should she refuse to give heed to this warning and domand, it would
not be necessary to deolare war, but to sever all diplomatic relations with Japan, and to
place an embargo upon all forms of trading, or commercial and monetary transactions.
I had simply a routine reply to this letter, and on Ootober 1, 1937, I wrote
again emphasising that "Time is of the essence", and that our Government should take
action. Still nothing was done,
894.24/329
On September 1, l9ss, I wrote again calling attention to my previpus letters,
stating that a whole year had passed, and the most horrible, barbarous warfare kmown in
civilised times had been carried on by Japan, murdering thousands upon thousands of
umprotected, unarmed civilians, raping wholesale girls and women of conquered territory,
with an utterly contemptuous disregard for the horror of the civilised world, especially
as no active measures were taken to even hamper her in her determination to subjugate 
China. Still no action was taken by our Government.
x
2
The United States is part of the world, one of the greatest nations of the worId
States has permitted Japan to flout the Nine-Power Treaty and, although unjustifiable 
massacres, death and destruetion are shocking the civilised world, there have been no
strong official protests made to Japan, or effeotive measures taken to end such shameful
violation of human rights by this "outlaw" nation.
I am opposed to all forms of aggressive warfare, but I thought that the United
States was right when it went to the resoue of the Cuban people fron the cruelty of
Spain. I thought it was right when it joined to fight against German submarine fright-
fulness, and the ruthlessness of the German warlords. I thought it was wrong when

=== Page 408 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
 failed to protect the Armenian Christians from unprovoked massacres by the Mohammedan
Turks. I think it has done wrong in that for over two years it has done nothing to
compel Japan to put an end to her bloody aggressions upon helpless China.
To my letter of September 1, 1938, I received a reply from Hon. R. Walton Moere,
Counselor to the Department of State. In that letter Mr. Moore called my attention to
the fact that "Whenever the President shall find that there exists a state of war between
or among two or more foreign states he shall proclaim such fact. Should the President
issue a proelamation finding that a state of war exists between China and Japan the
export of arms, ammunition and implements of war to both those countries would immediately
be prohibited by the terms of the Neutrality Act. In the absence of suoh a proolamation,
howover, there exists nolegal authppity for preventing the export of arms either to both
those countries or to Japan alone. ****In deoiding whether he should invoke the Joint
Resolution in a partieular situation the President must take into account various factors
and particularly the broad, general interests of this country and its nationals. In the
present situation in the Far Bast, the Prosident has oonoluded that, in the light of all
the facts and ciroumstanoes, when weighed from a practical point of view, it would not
be to the best interests of this country and our people as a whole to invoke the pro-
visions of this Joint Resolution."
It has been a matter not only of surprise, but of amazement, that the President
has not found that "there oxists a state of war" between Japan and China, and that he
has not proolaimed that fact. The people of the United States know, and they have kmown
for over two years, that a most destructive and barbarous warfare has been carried on
by Japen against China. The people are obliged to ask: "How can the President be ignorant
of that fact, or, if not ignorant, how can he be sc indifferent; or how can he fail to
perform the positive duty laid upon him by the Joint Resolution of Congress approved
May 1, 1937, that he shall deolare the fact 'that war does exist'?"
The President has manifested a great desire for peace among the countries of
Europe, has even sent an Assistant Secretary of State to investigate the possibilities
of peace, and has sent a special representative to the Vatioan with the rank of Ambassa-
dor to collaborate in seouring peace. The President has expressed his sympathy for the
nations of Europe whioh have been the viotims of unprovoked aggression, and his condemma-
tion of suoh aggression. But China has a population almost equal to that of the antire
Continent of Europe. China is olearly the viotim of the most ruthless aggression. Why
has not the President protested vigorously against the outrages and the massaores perpe-
trated by Japan? What are "the best interests of this country and our people as a whole"
whioh justify the President's course?
If the President kmows the sentiment of the people of the United States he knows
that nearly all the great Church bodies stand aghast that our Government has done nothing
to put an ond to the outlawry of Japan, but on the contrary that our country by continuing
to trade with Japan has furnished her money for her mamufactured produots, and has also
furnished her sorep iron, automobiles, truoks, oil and other material. Without American

AN OPEN LETTER TO PRESIDENT ROOSEVELT.
By Bishop James Canron, Jr.
the President,
The White House,
April 18, 1940.
Washington, D. C.
Dear Mr. President:
I wrote to Socretary Hull on September 1, 1937, less than sixty days after Je-
pan, without justification, with shell and bomb, was carrying destruotion and
death to peace-loving China, extending her masseoras even to defenseless civilians
In my letter I emphasized the appeal of Generaliss imo Chiang Kai-Shek for the in
tervention of foreign powers to compel Japan to end her unprovoked warfare.Japan
is not only a signer of the Kellogg Peaco Paot, but also of the Nine-Power Trosty.
whioh binds tho sigmers to protect the sovereignty and territoriality of China.
Japan's ruthless oonquest of Manchuria branded her as an outlaw among the na-
tione. This new attempt at the spoliation and domination of a peaceful oountry,
whose sovereignty she is bound by treaty to protect, is an attack not only upon
China, but upon the interests of all the great nations which trade with China,
and which have oom-ercial, educational, medical and religious investments in that
country. The declared policy of the United States has been, and is now, the "Open
Door" in China. But Jupan, with callous contempt not only for China, but for the
rights and proporty of other nations, is compelling peaceful foreigners to leave
China to save their lives; to lave their properties to be destroyed or stolen by
Japan, and to terminato their business, oducational, medical and religi us ao-
tivities.
I insisted in that letter of September 1, 1937, that the United States, as a
signer of the Nine-Power Treaty, must declare to Japan that her warfare against
China must ceaso, and that should she refuso to give heed to this warning and de-
mand, it would not be necessary to declaro war, but to sever all diplomatic rela-
tions with Japan, and to place an embargo upon all forms of trading, or oommer-
cial and monetary transactions.
I had simply a routine reply to this letter, and on October 1, 1937, I wrote
again emphasizing that "Time is of tho essence", and that our Government should
take aotion. Still nothing was dono.
On September 1, 1938, I wr te again calling ttontion to my previous letter,
stating that a whole yeur had passed, and tho m st horrible, barbarous warfare
known in civilized times had been carried on by Japan, murdering thousand upon
thousands of unprotocted, unarmod civilians, raping wholesalo girls and women of
conquered territory, with an utterly contemptuous disregard for the horror of the
civilized werld, especially us no active measures were taken to even hamper her
in her determination to subju ate China. Still no action was taken by our
Government.
The United States is part of the world, one of the greatest nations of the
w rld, and, as suoh, has to bear a great responsibility. For over two and one-
half years tho Unitad States has permitted Japan to flout tho Nine-Power Treaty
and, although unjustifiable massacres, death and destruction are shocking the
oivilized world, there have boen no strong offioial protests made to Japan, or
effective measures taken to end such shamoful violation of human rights by thi
"outlaw" nation.
I am opposed to all forms of aggressive warfare, but I thought the United
States was right when it went t, the rescue of the Cuban people from the cruelty
of Spain. I thought it was right when it joined to fight aguinst German subma-
rine frightfulnese, and the ruthlessness of the German warlords. I thoight it was
wrong when it failed to protect the Armenian Christians from unprovoked masscores
by the Mohammodan Turke. I think it has done wrong in that for over two years
it has done nothing to compel Japan to put an end to hor blondy aggressina upon
helpless China.
To my letter of Septembor 1, l938, I received a renly from Hon. R. Walton Moore.
Counselor to the Department of State. In that letter Mr. Moore called my attention
to the fact that "Whenever the President shall find that there exists a state of
war between or among two or more foreign states he shall proolaim suoh fact Should
the President issue a proolamation finding t at suoh a state of war exists
Chinm and apan the export of arms, ammunition and implomonts of war to both
riee would immediately be prohibited by the terms of the Noutrality

It has been a matter not only of surprise, but of amazement, that the President

WILBUR WATSON AND ASSOCIATES
