April 15 1940
Roger S Greene, Esq
#8 West 40th Street
New York City
Dear Mr Greene:
I appreciate your letter of April 5th and am very much interested in
your views upon some of the questions which I had tried to arouse
Col Stimson to discuss publioly.
There are some further comments which I should like to make but first
1t would perhaps be best to try to define my own position in the
whole matter because it is somewhat upon my conscience.
In 1931, when Japan first moved into Manchuria, I felt strongly that
we shouid do something more than simply frown, Before a number of
groups in this district I tried to bring out the true picture of what
was getting under way - tried to arouse some demend for an embargo
against our sale of war materials to Japan. The effort was not much
of a success - no one seemed to be interested in anything going on so
far away.
By 1934 and during my succeeding years with the Department of Commerce
- at least up until early in 1938 - it seemed rather hopeless to
agitate for an embargo against shipments of scrap iron, tools,
machinery petroleum products, etc. to Japan. There was too desperate
a need bere at home for the labor involved in manufacturing for export
and it seemed clear then that our refusal to furnish these things
would simply force Japan to go elsewhere for them. I even mgued half
heartedly along these lines with our own Department officials and with
the State Department advisors, feeling certain that no economic
blookade of Japan could be effective without definite cooperation from
England and other European powers and being certain that such co-
operation would not be forthcoming.
Since then, world conditions have so radically cianged that, to me
the whole picture with reference to our support of Japan is entirely
different. I regret my former passive attitude not because of any
idea that aggressive action on my part would have affected the
situation materially but beemse it now appears that such inaction was
wrong from almost every standpoint.
I ean agree with almost all of your observations concerning the presemt
simatien. It is my feeling that in recent months the chance of a
"awdden collision with Russis" has decreased and that Japan bas beem
able to pull back some of her crack troops from the Biberian border.
The develepments in Europe during this present week have probably
relieved ber anxiety still further in that quarter.
Just hew much reserve supply Japan now has of sorap, copper, alumimm,

=== Page 416 === (confidence 0.98)
一 2 
time Japan could continue her operations after our resources are
closed to her. Certainly other sources of these necessities are
largely out of reach now.
I agree in theory with the prinoipal which you say Secretary Hull
has emphasized - that wthe larger American interest is in the main-
taining of orderly relations between states" and I believe that his
work during recent years has been the one outstanding effort in the
whole world toward a return to sanity and health in international
political and trade relations. But the tide againat his ideals has
been too strong and certainly today the situation is so out of hand in
Burope and the Far East that our own moves should be tempered by
practical considerations along with theoretical 1deals.
Brom a cold-blooded, realistic point of view it seems to me tmt the
main outlines of the situation might be sketched in as followss - Since
the start of the present European war, Japan has been directly and
definitely dependent upon us for the essential war materials with which
to continue her aggression in China. Under present conditions she
cannot get these materials in sufficient quantities from any other
sourge at any price. It is clear therefore that we are Japan's
parthors in her attempts to achieve her objectives in China and the
rest of Asia. By our actions, which speak to the whole world, we are
indonsing these objectives and the methods used to attain them.
What these objectives really are is now established beyond any question
of doubt - for the present, & complete monopoly over Asias raw
materials and unhampered domination of her markets for products manu-
factured from these raw materials. But her plans do not s top there -
she envisions the time when, with these cheap raw materials, including
Chimese coal and metals, and the enslaved labor of half a billion
conquored Asiatics, her well organized industrial machine will be able
to produce in such abundance and at such low costs that she can easily
dominate the markets of the world.
It seems to me that there are two compelling reasons why the United
States should take action immediately:
lst. Japan's methods in attemping to gain her objectives in China are
such that no American citizen, with the true facts before his, could
possihly sanotion our support of them. The wholesale murder and
pillage and the ruthless destruction going on in China are definitely
gur affair so long as we continue as Japan's chief partner and supply
her with the materials and money she needs to contimue them and which
she pan get from no one alse.
In shite of the fact that we are co-signers with Japan of a certain
the obligation not to sive active support to the sort of international
radketeering and thaggery that is going on in the Far East today.
and. Entirely apart from any moral considerations, our contimuamee er
dsperately needed material aid to Japan toward attainins her objeet-
and illogical to an extreme degree.

=== Page 417 === (confidence 0.97)
1ready our scrap-iron and high grade steels, our machine-tools and
produetion equipment and our purehases of gold and silk have helped
Japan rise to a commanding position in the Orient - a position of
strong competition against us in many export markets - a position
demanding and getting huge increases in our own outlay for naval
strength and for air bases and fortifications.
If our continued help does carry Japan thru to success in her Asiatie
objeotives it will meen not just the loss of our Chinese export trade
- it will enable Japan to divorce herself entirely from the United
States as a source of strategic materials, to elbow us out of all
Asiatic and South American markets and to monopolize to ber own use
the essential raw materials of the Orient including rubber, tin, tung-
oil, etc. which are vital to our peace time economy. And it will
force us to expansions in our national defences out of all proportions
to the current profits accruing from our temporary boom - trade in the
export of war materials.
Curtatlment of that trade is being vigorously epposed in certain
quarters for it means employment and profits to a good many workers
and industries. But may not that temporary employment and the current
profits be at a price which in the long run will prove far to dear?
Actually if Japan is to win thru this help, it 1s not hard to prove
that it would be infinitely cheaper in ultinate doller outlay if our
government would purchase and store all exportable surpluses of serep
iron, metals and petroleum products - subsidize our siik processing
industries for their conversion to the use of synthetics and close
out completely our absurd partnership with Japan in exploits whieh
are contrary to all of the tenets of common decency and fair inter-
national dealings - a partnership which may ultimately prove ruinously
expensive and which certainly has great potentialities for dragging us
into serious complications in the Far East.
Therefore I am anxious to see some restrictive action taken at once. I
do not believe now that even the strictest sort of an embargo would
sause Japan to declare war against us and I doubt if it would heve amy
appreciable effect on her plans toward further alliances or aggression
against Bnglish and Dutch Indies.
But as to what can be done under existing law there seems to be a lot
of uncestainty. I believe that competent State Department officials
consider even the present restriction against free export of tin
has before it more complete facts on all sides of the question than
are available elsewhere and very liely would not welcome suggestion
from'outsiders.
It would seem to me that greater general publicity as to the tre
 interested than formerly.
Amy suggestions whioh you care to make would be appreciated.
Yours very truly
(sigmed)
